*...along a direct path from the beginning to the end. Because the reader is in no danger of losing his way, there is little need to provide him with additional guidance <note>original: "supernumerary lights"</note> in an introduction like this.*

*But since we have decided to write a preface, it might be worth noting that very few books contain such a vast variety of information within such a small space. The author appears to be a man of extensive reading and deep thought, as most of the books he cites are both rare and fascinating <note>original: "curious"</note>. However, unlike many German authors, he does not merely decorate his margins with citations—a practice sometimes done by simply copying the references found in other writers' works. Instead, he generally provides the actual passages that support his arguments, and often includes descriptions <note>original: "Characters"</note> of the authors themselves. This alone makes his work very useful to anyone wanting to learn about the value of books that are seldom found, and which, for that very reason, have reached a high price.*

*Another remarkable feature of this treatise is the choice the author has*

# THE PREFACE.

...made of his quotations, which are as entertaining as they are instructive. It is not easy to imagine, without reading his book, how it is possible for a man to enliven such a dry subject in the way he has done. The extracts he has provided from Roger Bacon <note>original: "Bacon the Monk," referring to the 13th-century philosopher and scientist</note> clearly show the immense knowledge of that extraordinary person. At a time when all of Europe was shrouded in the thickest fog of ignorance, he alone seems to have possessed such a treasure trove of science as would have made him a very significant man even in the most enlightened ages. This may possibly inspire some capable writer to provide us with an abridgment of his works, similar to what the clever and learned Dr. Peter Shaw <note>original: "Doctor Shaw"</note> has done for the works of Francis Bacon <note>original: "Chancellor Bacon"</note> and the famous Robert Boyle. If such a task were carefully performed and illustrated with proper notes, it would be of great service to the community of scholars <note>original: "Republic of Letters"</note> and bring honor to our country.

## *Hermippus Redivivus:* <note>original: "Hermippus Redivivus" translates from Latin as "Hermippus Restored to Life" or "Hermippus Reborn."</note>

### OR, THE

## Art of Preserving YOUTH.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial letter 'I' set within a square frame. The 'I' is flanked by two small figures (putti) holding a floral garland above the letter and surrounded by foliage and scrollwork.</image-desc>

IT was the praiseworthy custom of the ancients to preserve the memory of all remarkable <note>original: "singular"</note> events—and especially those that might in any way be useful to future generations <note>original: "Posterity"</note>—through inscriptions. These had a specific style in which three things were primarily valued: brevity <note>original: "Succinctness"</note>, elegance, and clarity. It would have been impractical, and indeed absurd, to make such pieces very long, considering the places where they were set up—namely, markets, temples, or public roads—or the material they were carved upon, which was marble or some other kind of hard or rare <note>original: "curious"</note> stone. But, if brevity was necessary, beauty was also appropriate. Where there is nothing striking,

<summary>This page introduces the treatise by discussing the ancient Roman custom of using concise, elegant inscriptions on stone to record important events for the benefit of future generations.</summary>
<keywords>inscriptions, ancients, posterity, brevity, Hermippus Redivivus, preservation of history</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient inscriptions, focusing on their stylistic origins in the East, Greece, and Rome, and their importance as clear records of knowledge.</meta>

the memory soon loses a thought. To retain what we are told, we must receive both pleasure and instruction at the same time. This elegance <note>original: "neatness"</note> of style <note>original: "Stile"</note> was especially cultivated by the ancients <note>original: "Antients"</note>. It originated in the *East*, where all knowledge <note>original: "Science"</note> was taught through parables and proverbs. In *Greece*, they refined this approach and established the fundamentals <note>original: "Rudiments"</note> of knowledge in aphorisms. Among the *Romans*, this way of teaching was highly valued, and nothing was more admired—even in the most sophisticated <note>original: "politest"</note> eras—than moral maxims <note>original: "Sentences," referring to pithy philosophical statements or "sententiae"</note>. If they were later misused and applied incorrectly, this should not discredit them, since without a doubt they always have, and always will, appear with perfect appropriateness in the kind of writing I am discussing. Finally, regarding clarity <note>original: "Perspicuity"</note>, the ancients were extremely careful; the difficulties that now arise concerning the true meaning of the scholarly records <note>original: "Monuments of their Learning"</note> that have survived to our time are due more to the unfortunate loss of accounts of their customs and manners—which would have made them easy and familiar to us—than to any lack of clarity in the style of their inscriptions.

MEN of various professions have studied these large and interesting <note>original: "curious"</note> collections with different goals in mind—collections that learned and hard-working people have gathered from such remains

<summary>The author explains that the concise and elegant style of ancient inscriptions evolved from Eastern parables and Greek aphorisms into Roman moral maxims. He argues that any current difficulty in understanding these inscriptions stems from a loss of historical context rather than a lack of clarity in the original writing.</summary>

<keywords>aphorisms, Roman maxims, clarity, ancient inscriptions, preservation of knowledge, stylistic evolution</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the examination of the inscription regarding L. Clodius Hermippus (or Hirpanus) and his longevity, specifically comparing the variations found in the accounts of different scholars like Cujas and the commentators on Pliny.</meta>

[ 4 ]

The famous Cujas <note>Jacques Cujas (1522–1590), a celebrated French legal scholar and expert in Roman law</note> gives it to us in the following manner:

->*L. Clodius Hirpanus,*<-
->*Lived 115 years and 5 days.* <note>original: "Vixit annos CXV. dies V."</note><-
->*Nourished by the breath of boys ᵇ.* <note>original: "Alitus puerorum anhelitu"</note><-

These various readings, though it cannot be denied that they strongly affect the meaning <note>original: "Sense"</note>, do not, however, greatly concern the subject. The first <note>Referring to the version of the inscription on the previous page</note> tells us that *L. Clodius Hermippus* lived one hundred and fifteen years and five days by the breath of young women, which is worthy of the consideration of physicians and of posterity. According to the commentator upon *Pliny* <note>Pliny the Elder, author of "Natural History." The commentator is likely Jacques Daléchamp, mentioned on the previous page</note>, the name of this long-lived man <note>original: "Long-liver"</note> was not *Hermippus*, but *Hirpanus*, and the length of his life was one hundred and fifty-five years and five days, with this additional circumstance: that he did not live by the breath of young women, but of young men. The lawyer <note>Cujas</note> again reduces the life to the same length <note>original: "Standard"</note> and contends only that his name was *Hirpanus*, and that he received this extraordinary nourishment from the breath of youths.

If, therefore, we take this inscription as it stands by the consensus of all these writers, it informs us of a fact equally curious and important, namely:

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the inscription concerning Hermippus (or Hirpanus), moving from the textual variations of the name and age to a scientific and theological inquiry into the possibility of prolonging life.</meta>

->[ 5 ]<-

It is claimed that a certain person—regardless of whether his name was *Hermippus* or *Hirpanus*—reached a very advanced age by using the breath of young women or young men. Now, whether this was a real fact that actually occurred, or whether it was an invention of some malicious wit among the ancients intended to challenge the talents of future generations <note>original: "Posterity"</note>, I do not concern myself. It appears to me as a scientific problem <note>original: "Physical Problem"</note> which may be expressed in very few words: namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, can the breath of young women likely contribute to maintaining a long life and staving off old age? This is what I propose to examine; this is to be the subject of my discourse. If what I provide is entertaining and useful, the reader need not worry much about the truth or falsehood of the inscription.

<margin>Manasseh ben Israel, *On the Term of Life* <note>original: "c Manaf. ben Israel de term. vitæ." Manasseh ben Israel (1604–1657) was a prominent rabbi and author who wrote on the theological question of whether the span of human life is predestined.</note></margin>

But before we come to a strict examination of this proposition, it will be necessary to clear away some preliminary difficulties. First, therefore, let us inquire whether the span of life <note>original: "Term of Life"</note> is a fixed or flexible <note>original: "moveable"</note> thing. Some of the most learned among the Jews have thought it absolutely determined by the decree of God, and have cited various scriptures as proof of this. The most

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether the human lifespan is fixed or flexible, moving from Jewish theological perspectives to the views of ancient Chaldean, Egyptian, and Stoic philosophers who believed in fate and astrological influence.</meta>

->[ 6 ]<-

Ancient philosophers, both in **Chaldea** and **Egypt**, thought that human life depended upon the stars. By pretending to predict its duration, they plainly showed that they thought it was absolutely under the influence of the heavenly bodies <margin>d. Diodorus Siculus, *History*, Book 1; Diogenes Laertius.</margin>. The **Stoics**—if they meant the power of the stars by what they called *Fate*—were also of this opinion. But whatever they meant by *Fate*, it is most evident that they thought human life, as well as every other thing, depended upon it <margin>e. Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Plut. de placit. Philosoph."</note>; Diogenes Laertius; Seneca.</margin>; and that consequently, it was unalterable by any means within the reach of human power. Now, if there is any truth in these opinions—if there is the least foundation for any one of them—the case is clear that all further inquiries are pointless. For to what end should we search out the most probable means of extending life, when we are certain that this is a thing beyond our capacity? Or at least, when it is uncertain whether it is or not?

In order to open the way to further speculations upon this curious and important subject, I will begin with the solution to this difficulty. I will take it upon myself to prove that, contrary to the notions of these theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note> and philosophers, there is no such thing as a settled term of life set by the law of nature—or, which is the same thing in other words, by the express will of Divine Providence.

<summary>The author examines the ancient belief that human life is governed by fate or the stars, arguing that if life were truly unalterable, any attempt to extend it would be futile. He intends to prove that the lifespan is not fixed by nature or God.</summary>

<keywords>Chaldea, Egypt, Astrology, Stoics, Fate, Divine Providence, Lifespan, Diodorus Siculus, Plutarch, Seneca</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument against a fixed lifespan, specifically focusing on the role of contingency in divine providence and scriptural evidence from the Old Testament.</meta>

Providence: In the first place, I lay it down as an absolute certainty that contingency <term>contingency: the idea that events occur by chance or depend on other factors, rather than being strictly predetermined</term> is essential to the way the divine power manages earthly things <note>original: "sublunary Things," referring to the world below the moon, i.e., the Earth</note>. Causes do indeed necessarily produce their effects; however, I deny that there is any unbreakable chain of necessary causes. Because it would require much time and space to establish this doctrine generally, I will keep close to my subject and content myself with proving it regarding this particular point. 

It is universally agreed that the Law of *Moses* primarily offered earthly blessings <note>original: "Temporal Blessings"</note>, and among these was long life. Now, this is absolutely incompatible with the doctrine of a fixed lifespan. Therefore, if there are any passages in the Scripture that seem to suggest otherwise, we must interpret them in a different sense; for the specific part must agree with the whole, and it is pointless to cite a few isolated passages against the design of the entire Book. 

Again, we are told that God commended *Solomon* for praying to Him for wisdom rather than for long life or riches. This plainly shows that he might just as easily have asked for either of those as for wisdom. But if this inference is not convincing, consider God’s express decision on the matter; in the same passage, He says that if *Solomon* walked in the ways of his father *David*, He would lengthen his days <margin>f 1 Kings 3:11, 14</margin>. Add to

<summary>The author argues that human lifespan is not fixed, citing the Mosaic Law's promises of long life and God's offer to lengthen King Solomon's life as evidence that life expectancy depends on one's actions rather than a predetermined fate.</summary>
<keywords>Providence, contingency, sublunary things, Law of Moses, temporal blessings, Solomon, King David, 1 Kings, free will vs. fate, biblical interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument against the idea of a predetermined, unchangeable lifespan, concluding that such a concept contradicts both human reason and scriptural examples.</meta>

...these arguments: that it was the constant practice of the best men—and those who most intimately knew the will of God—to pray in terms incompatible with such an absolute decree. For instance, *Hezekiah* desired that his life might be spared <gloss>g</gloss>. *Elijah* <note>original: "Elias"</note> <gloss>h</gloss> and *Jonah* prayed that theirs might be shortened <gloss>i</gloss>.

But, to put the matter beyond doubt, one only needs to consider the choice given to *David* by the prophet *Nathan* <gloss>k</gloss>: a choice between war, pestilence, or famine. In response, *David* chose pestilence. Now, if there had been a fixed time of death for every person, how could this choice have possibly mattered? According to the doctrine I am refuting, all the people who died of the pestilence <term>pestilence: a fatal epidemic disease, often referring to the bubonic plague</term> would have died even if there had been no pestilence, because the decreed term of their lives would have expired anyway. 

On the whole, therefore, it is apparent that a fixed term of life is inconsistent with the "reason of things." This is what we call the Law of Nature, wherein—if I may be allowed the expression—there is a "settled contingency" <term>settled contingency: the philosophical idea that while the laws of nature are constant, specific outcomes depend on various shifting factors rather than being strictly predetermined</term>. This doctrine of a fixed lifespan is contrary to the principles of religion as deduced from the natural light of our understandings <note>original: "natural Light of our Understandings," a common Enlightenment phrase referring to human reason and logic</note>, and it is directly contrary to divine Revelation. Therefore, I take it for granted that any pious person who seriously considers these arguments must remain...

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the idea that humans have a predetermined time of death. Having addressed theological arguments on the previous page, the author now turns to debunking astrological claims.</meta>

satisfied that the Order of Providence, the Will of Heaven, or God's Decree—which are the terms used by theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note> on this subject—has determined nothing absolutely concerning the length <note>original: "Term"</note> of life.

I am next to speak of the opinions of the astrologers, which in ancient times were attributed to *Aesculapius* <note>the ancient Greek god of medicine</note>, who, it is said, established this rule <note>original: "Maxim"</note>: that all things on earth <note>original: "below"</note> were governed by the motions and alignments <note>original: "Aspects"</note> of the stars. Against this notion, I have two things to offer. The first is that they could not possibly know this to be true, even granting it should be true, because they were absolutely ignorant of the true system of the heavens and of the motions of those bodies from which they would derive such a great power. This removes their authority from the matter; for if we cannot depend upon them as astronomers, nothing in nature can be more ridiculous than to pretend to place any importance <note>original: "stress"</note> on their judgment in astrology. To be convinced of this, we need only consult the authentic accounts we have in ancient authors of the <term>Chaldaic System</term> <note>the astrological and astronomical beliefs of the ancient Babylonians</note> ¹; from which it appears to be equally false and absurd. It is false, as it is contrary to the experience and observation of succeeding times; and it is absurd, as it is contrary to the unalterable principles of reason and true science.

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument against the astrological and philosophical claims that human life spans are predestined. He transitions from debunking ancient authorities to addressing modern thinkers who might still support such views.</meta>

The other argument I offer <note>original: "allege"</note> is this: since the authority of the ancients is irrelevant <note>original: "out of the case"</note>, if there are any modern *Stoics* <note>philosophers who believe in fate and that the universe is governed by necessity</note> who still hold this opinion, they must show us the evidence <note>original: "grounds"</note> for it. This evidence must not come from the imaginary <note>original: "chimerical"</note> notions set forth by the so-called <note>original: "pretended"</note> experts in <term>judicial astrology</term> <note>the practice of predicting individual fates or earthly events based on the stars</note>, but from the established and unquestioned principles of true philosophy. When they do this—or at least, when they attempt to do it—it will be time enough to examine how far this new system of theirs is rational. In the meantime, I will conclude this point with that established and most reasonable principle <note>original: "maxim"</note> of <term>civil law</term>: *concerning things that do not exist, and things that cannot be proven to exist, the reasoning is the same* <note>original Latin: "De non apparentibus et non existentibus eadem est ratio"</note>. That is, there can be no reasoning about them; they must be viewed in the same way, since nothing is more pointless than to argue about the consequences of a thing before the thing itself is proven or we have any real reason for a dispute.

We have now made some progress toward establishing the foundation of our theory <note>original: "doctrine"</note>, but there is still another common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> notion in our way that must be removed—or to speak more accurately, explained. By this, I mean the prevailing opinion that the life of man is limited to a fixed length <note>original: "term"</note>. Because we see people grow old at a certain age—by which I mean, grow infirm...

<summary>The author challenges modern thinkers to provide rational, philosophical proof for a predetermined lifespan rather than relying on astrology. He concludes that until such proof is provided, the idea of a fixed life term should be treated as non-existent.</summary>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument by refining the definition of "old age" and shifting the focus to the biological and divine design of the human body.</meta>

...infirm and decrepit; for oldness refers not so much to time as to one's condition. I consider a person old when they have lost their strength, rather than simply being advanced in years. However, because the ailments <note>original: "Maladies"</note> associated with old age appear in most people at a certain stage of life, they are considered inevitable. If there were any truth to this, we would be blocked again; it would be a new barrier through which we could find no passage. When we spoke of slowing down old age, people would expect us to make them young again. It is necessary, therefore, that this matter be explained and set right, so that the reader is satisfied regarding how many of the disadvantages <note>original: "incommodities"</note> of old age are inevitable, and how many are the result of natural causes that can be influenced by the skill and knowledge <note>original: "Art"</note> of man.

The human body is a machine, powered by an immortal spirit and—which is more to my purpose—created by an almighty hand. It is inconceivable that this dwelling place should be so flimsy or so poorly designed that it would wear out in a very short amount of time. This does not seem consistent with the nature of man, considered as a rational creature, or with the infinite skill and wisdom evident in the design <note>original: "Composition"</note> of the human body <note>original: "Frame"</note>. Therefore, when theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note> say that **Sin** introduced **Death**, they seem to be speaking **philosophically**;

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>The author argues that old age should be defined by physical condition rather than years and suggests that because the human body is a divine and sophisticated "machine," its early decay is not an inevitable necessity but a condition that can be mitigated by human skill.</summary>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that death and disease are not inherent parts of the human design, but rather consequences of human behavior and lifestyle choices.</meta>

And if I may be allowed to explain their meaning, I am inclined to say that diseases and death do not happen <note>original: "are not incident"</note> to the human body by the will of God—or what is the same thing, by the law of nature. Instead, they were brought upon us <note>original: "superinduced"</note> by the follies and vices of men, which naturally carry the seeds of death within them. Therefore, if death is an evil, we should not blame divine providence <note>original: "Providence"</note>, but ourselves.

Yet I will readily acknowledge that, given how things are now and have been for many ages, there is no hope left for immortality in this body, or even of prolonging our lives to three or five hundred years. But I still maintain that there are no fixed periods in nature, nor any inevitable laws, that link weakness and physical decline <note>original: "infirmity"</note> to a specific number of years. Instead, it is very possible—and even practical—for a person to extend the length of their life far beyond the common span. This can be done without suffering the discomforts <note>original: "incommodities"</note> of old age; otherwise, this would be more like avoiding death than truly preserving life.

To prove this, I will first use logic <note>original: "Reason"</note> and then experience. I will use arguments to show that it *can* be so, and examples <note>original: "Instances"</note> to show that it *has been* so. Then, if any skeptic chooses to doubt the truth of my teaching <note>original: "Doctrine"</note>, I will leave him to his own path, where his ignorance will inevitably bring upon

<meta>catchword: him</meta>

<keywords>longevity, providence, law of nature, life extension, morality and health, skepticism</keywords>

...him the just punishment of his obstinacy: a quicker <note>original: "hastier"</note> extinction of life than he might otherwise have suffered.

All wise men have agreed that Nature—by which I mean the wisdom of God manifested in the order of all things—acts uniformly in every respect and assigns proper periods to all things. *Solomon says, "There is a time to be born, and a time to die"*; <note>Ecclesiastes 3:2</note> this is a short maxim which could allow for a long commentary. The life of man certainly does not consist only of eating and drinking, waking or sleeping, or in taking turns indulging the gratification of his many appetites. If this were so, the usual length <note>original: "Term"</note> of life would certainly be long enough. Indeed, there have actually been instances of people who were so fed up <note>original: "fatiated"</note> with the cycle of these trivial actions that they grew weary of life before death was near, and as a result of this weariness, they hastened its arrival. 

But as that "divine old man" <note>Referring to the ancient Greek physician Hippocrates</note> justly observes, *Art is long, and life is short*; <note>original Latin: "Vita brevis, ars longa..."</note> that is, there seems to be no proper proportion between the powers of the mind and the strength <note>original: "Force"</note> of the body. We hurry on from infancy to childhood; from childhood to manhood <note>original: "the Age of Man"</note>; and from there, to what is called middle...

***

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the rapid decline of the human body in middle age is not a necessary law of nature, but rather a result of human failing.</meta>

middle-aged, and then we decline quickly <note>original: "a-pace"</note> into feebleness, misery, and senility <note>original: "dotage"</note>. Can any man think that this is the true order of Nature? Has Nature given so many years to pikes, to eagles, and to stags—even to serpents—at the same time that she denies them to man? Let it be far from us to believe this; let us instead suppress our pride and conceit, and believe that the shortness of life flows from the weakness and wickedness of man.

BUT it will be said that death is not only the end of diseases, but that old age is as much a disease as any other, and certainly brings death on. It is argued that the human body <note>original: "Frame"</note> is so constructed as to not only grow and arrive at perfection, but also to decay and wear out. They say that the flame of life grows weaker and weaker after a certain time; that the solid parts of the body lose their vigor <note>original: "Tone"</note> by degrees, and that the vessels grow cartilaginous <term>cartilaginous: the process of tissues hardening into cartilage, often associated with aging in early medical theory</term> and finally become bony; so that death by old age is a natural thing °. Let it be so; I am not arguing that men may live forever, or even that they may live (as I said before) for three or five hundred years. But I do say that old age is the only disease to which we are subject by nature, and that it is very possible for men to be protected from it much longer than they usually are through the help of medical science <note>original: "Art"</note>.

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that human longevity is limited by lack of medical knowledge and care, rather than by a fixed divine or natural law. He asserts that the body, as a "machine," should theoretically last much longer.</meta>

->[ 15 ]<-

This I say, principally on the grounds I have previously established: namely, that the human body is an admirably constructed machine. From this, I infer that with proper care, it can be kept in good working order. Furthermore, since the true purposes of life require much more time than the normal lifespan allows, it is more likely that this unreasonable brevity of life is the result of our lack of skill, care, or attention, rather than any law established by the omniscient <note>all-knowing</note> Creator <note>original: "Author of all Things"</note>.

These are my principles, which I submit to the strictest examination. If they can be proven to be either false or uncertain <note>original: "precarious"</note>, I will be sorry for myself and for mankind. Undoubtedly, these principles strongly appear to be true—and they represent the most pleasing kind of truth: the kind that gives glory to God by demonstrating His goodwill toward humanity. 

But lest anyone should say that many things look good in theory <note>original: "Speculation"</note> but fail us in practice, I will follow this logical reasoning <note>original: "Rays of Reason"</note> by examining my doctrine through the light of experience. Surely, if experience shows no flaws in this argument, the reader and I shall proceed cheerfully on our journey. He will no longer view me as someone merely amusing him with a learned paradox, but as the revealer of a great and useful truth.

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s transition from theoretical reasoning to historical evidence. Having argued that the human "machine" should theoretically last longer, he now addresses the historical precedent of long-lived individuals in the Bible and ancient history to prove that a short lifespan is not an inevitable law of nature.</meta>

->[ 16 ]<-

->**AGAINST the common Opinion**<-

Contrary to common opinion, the most authentic historical records allow me to argue that although everyday experience contradicts my views, the shortness of human life that is now common was not always so, nor is it the case in all places today. 

I will not dwell long on what is said regarding the great ages of the antediluvian patriarchs <note>original: "Antidiluvian Patriarchs"; these are the biblical figures, such as Methuselah, recorded as living for several centuries before the Great Flood</note>. I will satisfy myself with only a few observations necessary to my purpose, the truth of which cannot be disputed. 

First, let it be noted that although men then lived to be very old, the species was new. The human body had only recently been fashioned from the earth by its Creator and therefore retained a great deal of its original <note>original: "primitive"</note> strength. In our own times, we commonly say that a man who "lives fast" <note>meaning to live a life of excess, indulgence, or high stress</note> works to destroy his own constitution <note>his physical health and vitality</note>. According to the principles of Moses <note>the traditional author of Genesis</note>, we must allow that the structure <note>original: "Fabric"</note> of Adam’s body must have been much stronger and better constructed than ours. This strength is what preserved him for so long, even after he had lost the "Tree of Life" or that rule of living which was suited to his original state. 

My second observation is that these patriarchs lived in another world—by which I mean a world structured differently than ours and functioning in a different way. Therefore, what we are told of their age is no more incredible than many other facts which experience and evidence force us to believe.

<summary>The author argues that human longevity was historically much greater than it is today, citing the biblical patriarchs as evidence. He suggests that Adam's body was naturally more durable as a new creation and that the environment of the pre-flood world was fundamentally different.</summary>

<keywords>longevity, Adam, Moses, antediluvian patriarchs, human constitution, Tree of Life, biblical history, physical strength</keywords>

It is very remarkable that not only the sacred writers but all the ancient **Chaldean**, **Egyptian**, and **Chinese** authors speak of the great ages of those who lived in early times. They do this with such confidence that **Xenophon**, **Pliny**, and other sensible people accept their testimony without hesitation. But to come down to later times: **Attila**, King of the **Huns**, who reigned in the fifth century, lived to 124 years old. He then died of over-indulgence on the first night of his second wedding to one of the most beautiful princesses of that age <margin>q</margin>. **Piastus**, King of **Poland**, who was raised from the rank of a peasant to that of a prince in the year 824, lived to be 120. He governed his subjects with such ability until the very end that his name is still held in the highest honor among his countrymen. <note>The original text ends with the catchword "trymen," signaling the completion of the word "countrymen" on the next page.</note>

This is credible, since in the parliament <note>original: "Diet," a formal assembly of estates</note> assembled by the late Queen of *Sweden* in 1713, the boldest and best speaker among the deputies from the social class <note>original: "Order"</note> of peasants was well over a hundred years old ʰ. But I limit myself to such accounts as are truly reliable. I gather these examples not to show off my own learning or merely to satisfy the reader's curiosity, but to prove a point: since the fact is certain that humans can far exceed what are considered the normal limits of life, this blessing of longevity is not restricted to any single climate or region. Therefore, we should not despair of finding out the science <note>original: "Art"</note> of prolonging life and warding off the decline of old age in any country.

Despite everything I have said, it may perhaps be suggested that in a matter like this—which so deeply affects humanity and in which every person would naturally be so interested—if such a thing were at all possible, this science would have been discovered long ago. This would seem especially likely since, in every age, there has been a group of men—namely, physicians—whose specific business it was to study such discoveries. <meta>This page continues the author's response to skeptics who argue that if the science of longevity were possible, it would have been discovered long ago. He counters by citing historical figures who claimed to possess this knowledge and introduces Roger Bacon's theories on the subject.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>[ 21 ]</page-num>

<margin>Sabellicus <note>original: "Sab. bel." likely referencing the works of Marcantonio Sabellico</note>, Book 10, Chapter 8, page 69.</margin>
greatest obstacles to knowledge <note>original: "Bars to Science"</note>, that many useful arts are but of very late invention; and that if this science had been discovered previously, it might have been concealed from posterity for very many reasons. But besides this, the reality is quite the opposite: this art was openly and diligently sought by the ancients, and some of them are said to have discovered it. **Asclepiades the Persian** <note>Though the author calls him Persian, this story is usually attributed to the Greek physician Asclepiades of Bithynia</note>, whom I have mentioned already, used to declare that he accounted any physician ignorant of his profession who could not defend himself from diseases. He supported this notion by his own example, having lived in the full enjoyment of health for 150 years, and was then unfortunately killed by a fall down stairs <gloss>i</gloss>. **Mithridates**, King of *Pontus*, also claimed to possess this secret, as did many others. But the best answer that can be given to all doubts of this kind may be found in the following passage from **BACON**, a famous *English* monk who lived in the 13th century <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292), a philosopher and friar who emphasized the study of nature through empiricism</note>, who discusses the subject in this way:

> “That it is possible to prolong life,” *he says*,
> “may thus be made evident. By nature, 
> man is immortal—that is to say, he was 
> originally formed in such a way that he 
> might have escaped dying; and even after 
> he had sinned, he could live a thousand

> “Therefore, it must be that this shortening of life <note>original: "Abbreviation"</note> is accidental; consequently, it may be either wholly repaired or at least partially. If we were to investigate the accidental cause of this physical decline <note>original: "Corruption," referring here to biological degeneration</note>, we would find it comes neither from Heaven nor from anything else but the lack of a proper health regimen <term>Regimen of Health</term><note>A systematic plan of diet, exercise, and lifestyle to maintain health.</note>. Since fathers are physically weakened, they produce children with a weakened constitution and physical makeup; those children, for the same reason, are weakened themselves. Thus, this physical decline is passed from father to son until the shortening of life prevails through successive generations.
>
> “Yet despite all this, it does not follow that life will always be cut shorter and shorter. This is because a limit is set for humanity: that men should, at the most, reach eighty years <note>original: "fourscore"</note> of age. However, what matters more is their health regimen, which consists of these things: food and drink, sleep and wakefulness, movement and rest, bodily elimination and retention <note>original: "Evacuation and Retention," referring to the body's intake and waste processes</note>, air, and the emotions of the mind.
> 
> “If a man were to observe this regimen from his birth <note>original: "Nativity"</note>, he might live as long as the nature inherited from his parents would permit. He might be led to the absolute limit of nature—that which has fallen from its original state of perfection <note>original: "lapsed from original Righteousness," a theological reference to the state of humanity after the Fall of Adam</note>. Nevertheless, he could not pass that limit, because this regimen does nothing to counteract the ancient physical decline inherited from our ancestors. But it being, in a way, impos-

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the properties of human breath, arguing that because air takes on the qualities of the body it passes through, an elderly person can absorb the vitality of youth by breathing the same air as young people.</meta>

[ 27 ]

Breath, by passing through the lungs, must be strongly saturated <note>original: "tinctured"</note> with the particles of that body through which it has passed; and when it mixes again with the atmosphere, it must communicate certain qualities which the air did not have before. This is so reasonable, and at the same time so self-evident, that I think it cannot be denied. From this it follows that where there are many people in one room, the air that is common to them all must be strongly impregnated with their breaths. If, therefore, an old man is surrounded for many hours by young people, we cannot help perceiving that he must take in a great quantity of that air which they have breathed out <note>original: "respired"</note>, and which consequently must be loaded with those particles which it carried off while passing through their lungs.

To better judge this matter, let us consider first the effect <note>original: "Action"</note> of odors in general upon the human body. <meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the physical and medicinal properties of breath, moving from ancient superstitions and tall tales to the practical and scientific distinctions in modern medicine.</meta>

They mention prayers which they claim were taught by **Saint Elmo**. The Catholic Church has condemned this fanatical practice, and so do I. However, there is another practice somewhat related to this which—whether nature or chance first introduced it into the world—is uncertain, but it is unlikely ever to go out of fashion. This is the practice of breathing on people in a <term>swoon</term> <note>a fainting spell</note> in order to bring them back to consciousness. This has been found to be very effective, as might well be expected from natural causes. In all cases of this nature, let us distinguish between reason and popular rumor, between the opinions of the common people and the judgment of capable authorities, and between the practices of <term>mountebanks</term> <note>charlatans or quack doctors</note> and deceivers and the deductions made from scientific principles by men of learning and sound sense.

I do not, however, deny that popular remedies have found admittance into <term>physic</term> <note>original: "physic"; the medical profession or the study of medicine</note> and ought to be kept there. In medicine, as well as in chemistry, the effects of medicines are treated as experiments upon which wise men reason and determine proper uses, even though a different sort of people might have first discovered them. It is one thing to prescribe medicine rationally and another to simply be lucky in curing a patient. **Friedrich Hoffmann** and **Hermann Boerhaave** <note>Hoffmann (1660–1742) and Boerhaave (1668–1738) were leading figures in 18th-century systematic medicine.</note> are as different as can be from **Paracelsus** and **Jan Baptist van Helmont**, who were nevertheless very extraordinary men in their own right.

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of the theory that the breath of young people can rejuvenate the elderly, using a fictionalized, Platonic allegory involving characters named Hermippus and Thysbe.</meta>

<page-num>[ 33 ]</page-num>

I do not imagine that *Hermippus* found this remedy against old age through abstract reasoning; I am inclined instead to think that it found him. That is to say, he was led to the cause by its vigorous effects, and by feeling himself revived by this medicine, he was taught its knowledge and efficacy. If one were disposed to follow the method of the ancients and to dress this story in the style that *Plato* would have given it, one might tell it this way:

WHEN the blooming *Thysbe*—whom the Graces adorn and the Muses instruct—converses with the good old *Hermippus*, her youth invigorates his age. The brisk flame that warms her heart communicates its heat to his; as often as the lovely young woman breathes, kind vapors fly off, full of the lively spirits <note>In 18th-century medicine, "spirits" (or vital spirits) were thought to be an extremely subtle, invisible fluid that transmitted life, heat, and motion through the body.</note> that swim in her purple veins. These, old *Hermippus* greedily breathes in; and as spirits quickly attract other spirits, they are immediately mingled with the old man’s blood. Thus the vapor, which only a moment before was expelled by the brisk beating of *Thysbe’s* heart, is communicated through the <term>ether</term> <note>original: "Æther"; in this context, the air or a refined medium thought to fill all space and transmit physical influences</note> to *Hermippus*. Passing through his heart, it serves to invigorate his blood, so that—almost without using a metaphor—we may say the spirits of *Thysbe* give life to *Hermippus*. For what is easier to understand than that the active spirits of this lively and blooming girl should, when received from the air, thaw the frozen juices of her aged

**Such** would have been its appearance if we had derived this fact from a *Greek Sage* <note>original: "Grecian Sage"</note>, and then perhaps it would have been honored with numerous commentaries and laborious explanations. But the *Romans* were a more serious people; they contented themselves with delivering truth in the language of truth, and thought that, like all great beauties, she looked best in a plain dress.

<margin>Pliny, *Natural History*, Book 7, Chapter 48. Valerius Maximus, Book 8, Chapter 13.</margin>
**History** informs us that very many people who spend their time in the instruction—and consequently in the company—of youth have lived to great ages. For example, *Gorgias*, the teacher of *Isocrates* and many other eminent persons, lived to be 108. The year before his death, someone in his school asked how he had been able to support the tedious burden of old age for so long. The Sophist <note>original: "Sophist"; in ancient Greece, these were professional teachers of rhetoric, philosophy, and virtue.</note> replied that he regretted nothing he had done and felt nothing of which he could reasonably complain. "My youth," he said, "cannot accuse me, nor can I accuse my old age."$^s$ His student *Iso-*

SUCH would have been its appearance if we had derived this fact from a Greek <note>original: "Grecian"</note> sage; then, perhaps, it would have been honored with numerous commentaries and labored explanations. But the Romans were a more serious <note>original: "graver"</note> people; they were satisfied with delivering truth in the language of truth, and thought that like all great beauties, she looks best in a plain dress.

HISTORY informs us that very many who spend their time in the instruction—and consequently in the company—of youth have lived to great ages. Thus Gorgias, the teacher <note>original: "Master"</note> of Isocrates and many other eminent persons, lived to be 108. The year before his death, someone in his school asked how he had been able to endure <note>original: "support"</note> the tedious burden of old age for so long. The sophist <note>sophist: an ancient Greek teacher of philosophy and rhetoric.</note> replied that he regretted nothing he had done and felt nothing of which he could reasonably complain. THE celebrated *Lewis Cornaro* <note>Luigi Cornaro (c. 1464–1566) was a Venetian nobleman famous for his "Discourses on the Sober Life," in which he detailed how a strict diet allowed him to recover his health and live to nearly one hundred years old.</note>, so well known to the world by his elaborate treatises on the benefits of a sober life, amongst other things that he relates of himself, tells us this: that when he first began his regimen, he took home eleven little nephews—all sons of the same father and mother, and all fine, healthy children—whom he took the pains to educate himself. He added that whenever he came from the Senate, he diverted himself with the innocent mirth, harmless sports, and inoffensive tattle <note>original: "Tatle"; meaning chatter or idle talk.</note> of the youngsters.

Some of the elder of them, says he, entertained me more agreeably. They understand music, often play upon the lute, and accompany it with their voices; and I too, he adds, frequently join my own, which is as clear, as strong, and as sweet as ever it was.

I have likewise, says he, composed a very diverting comedy, the scenes of which are varied with an inoffensive variety of wit and humor, featuring an agreeable turn of thought and cleverness <note>original: "quaintness"; in the 18th century, this often referred to ingenuity or refined elegance.</note> of expression. Comedy, you know, is generally the child of youth, as tragedy is the product of old age. The latter, on account of its gravity and seriousness, befits more mature years; while the former, by its gaiety of wit and humor, is more agreeable to youth. Now, if

<meta>This page continues the description of Francis Secardi Hongo (also known as Huppazoli), detailing his extraordinary health, his specific diet, and the author's theory that his long life was sustained by the company of young women.</meta>

[He was] never sick; his sight, hearing, memory, and physical activity were amazing. He walked about eight miles every day. His hair, which was long and graceful, turned white by the time he was eighty <note>original: "Fourscore"</note>, but it turned black again when he was a hundred, as did his eyebrows and beard at age 112. At 110, he lost all his teeth, but the year before he died, he grew <note>original: "cut"</note> two large ones with great pain.

His food generally consisted of a few spoonfuls of broth, after which he ate some small amount of roasted meat. His breakfast and supper were bread and fruit; his constant drink was distilled water, without any addition of wine or other strong liquor, until the very last. He was a man of strict honor and great abilities, with a free, pleasant, and sprightly temperament, as we are told by many travelers who were all struck by the good sense and good humor of this polite old man <gloss>See *The Travels of Tournefort*, etc. (original: *Voyages de Tournefort*)</gloss>.

The reader will easily see that the point I am chiefly emphasizing is that he was continually surrounded by young company, especially young women. For although neither he, nor as far as I know anyone else, ever remarked that this might contribute to the lengthening of his life, it may well have been the case, even if it went unnoticed. It is indeed more useful for my purpose to collect accounts like these, where there is clear evidence of the fact I wish to establish before the

I will suppose, then, regarding our *Hermippus*, that he was a tutor or director of a college of young women <note>original: "College of Virgins"</note>. Since the historical inscription does not tell us exactly what his role was, we may be allowed the freedom of a rational conjecture. I imagine this college of young women was founded purely for the sake of education, thereby providing a quick and constant succession of young girls <note>original: "little Maids"</note> between the ages of five or six and thirteen or fourteen. I imagine it might have been necessary for the health, as well as the proper instruction of these young ladies, that they rose quite early in the morning in all seasons of the year. He would be obliged to entertain them during the walk with appropriate <note>original: "seasonable"</note> and lively stories. <meta>This page continues the author's speculative reconstruction of how the Roman schoolmaster Hermippus lived his life among his young female students. It argues that storytelling is an ideal educational tool for young women because it combines imagination with moral examples.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>

Tales that show a liveliness of imagination <note>original: "Vivacity of Imagination"</note>, along with an occasional ray of sound judgment, are best suited for young minds, and especially for women’s intellects. Women are remarkably influenced by example—or, to use a modern phrase, by the fashion. Why, then, should they not be taught through examples? There is less difference than most people imagine between real and fictional stories. They differ only like morning and evening shadows: one is a faint picture of what has passed, while the other is a vivid representation of what might possibly happen. In all other respects, they are the same. For example, the historical story of **Alexander** and **Roxana** <note>Roxana was the wife of Alexander the Great; their marriage was a frequent subject of historical romance.</note> is now just as much a dream as any story in the *Persian Tales* <note>The *Persian Tales* (or *The Thousand and One Days*) was a popular 18th-century collection of stories, often compared to the *Arabian Nights*.</note>. 

One advantage that fictional accounts <note>original: "Ideal relations"</note> have over stories based on facts is that we can design them to avoid inappropriate details <note>original: "improper Circumstances"</note>. This is often difficult to do with history without sacrificing the truth. With such discussions, once their early morning prayers were over, these beautiful pupils could be entertained on winter mornings. In this way, the day would begin with a free, cheerful, and pleasant spirit, which was both enjoyable for his lovely students and satisfying for the good old man.

After this morning activity, I imagine that **Hermippus** and his female pupils go their separate ways to bathe, dress, and groom themselves, all...

Everyone knows that the ancients were remarkably fond of baths and unctions <note>original: "Unctions"; the practice of applying oils or ointments to the body for health or ritual purposes.</note>, especially for old men. The Arabian physicians <note>Medieval Islamic doctors like Avicenna or Rhazes, whose medical texts were highly influential in Europe during the Early Modern period.</note> prescribed them once a week or every ten days, and ordered that some hot and well-spiced meat be eaten after using them. 

Certainly, washing, cleansing, and keeping the body neat makes old men pleasant and agreeable to those who speak with them. It also contributes significantly to their own comfort and to preserving that flexibility <note>original: "Suppleness"</note> in the limbs which is frequently lost in old age. On the other hand, elegance in dress and great attention to physical hygiene <note>original: "Niceness about their Bodies"</note> are of very great importance for maintaining a proper disposition in these young maids, making them useful in the way we are discussing. 

Around noon, after all this exercise is finished, I imagine Hermippus meets his maidens with as much cheerfulness as his physical health <note>original: "Constitution"</note> allows, while they greet him with the liveliness natural to their youth. A short conversation should precede a light, well-chosen, and wholesome meal <note>original: "Entertainment"</note>. While everyone eats freely—but not to the point of being overfull—they season the necessary task of nourishing the body <note>original: "recruiting the Body"</note> with such clever and entertaining stories as may amuse and refresh the mind. After the meal, music...

The ladies, and especially the youngest among them, should be indulged in all the little innocent pastimes that contribute either to the exercise of their limbs or the improvement of their wits. After this, they might retire to their dormitory, where each has her own private room <note>original: "proper Cell"</note>, but all are open at the top—as is the director’s apartment at the upper end. 

In a college <note>A "college" here refers to a structured community or society living together under a specific set of rules.</note> like this, with such company and under such regulations—where all the pupils are as chaste as *Diana’s nymphs* <note>In Roman mythology, Diana was the goddess of the hunt and chastity; her nymphs were her virginal companions.</note>, as fresh as the spring, as sweet as the summer, and as harmless as the winter—they are always full of life and spirit. By this regimen <note>original: "Regimen"; a prescribed way of life or medical system.</note>, he might be provided with an almost continual refreshment from their fragrant breath <note>original: "Odoriferous Breaths"; the author believes that inhaling the breath of the young and healthy transmits "vital spirits" to the elderly.</note>, and never lose their company except at times appropriate for them and for him. Secluded in such a retreat, away from all the hurry, disorder, and accidents that attend a life of business, he would easily escape anxiety and fatigue. Removed from all considerations of profit, pomp, and pleasure, he would never feel the cruel disturbance of any restless passion. The desire for gain would never torment him, nor would he ever be perplexed by the fear of loss. If his heart were ever touched by pain, it would only be upon parting with a student <note>original: "Scholar"</note>; even this would be relieved by the pleasure he would feel in receiving a new disciple. At least, these are the comforts I have imagined for him; those of my readers who are blessed with more vigorous imaginations <note>original: "Fancies"</note> may conceive of him possessing even higher and more exquisite blessings.

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his theory regarding how Hermippus, a Roman man mentioned in an ancient inscription, lived to be 115 years old by surrounding himself with young women.</meta>

<page-num>[ 44 ]</page-num>

I am aware that it may be objected that this entire hypothesis is completely arbitrary—that among the *Romans*, there was never any such school<gloss>a</gloss> as this under the direction of any man, nor, as far as it appears, in any other nation. However, it should be considered that I have presented it as such. If *Hermippus* was truly nourished by the breath of young women—and as I have already shown, it is very reasonable to believe he might have been—then we must imagine for ourselves some occupation or way of life in which he could easily and constantly receive this pleasant medicine <note>original: "comfortable Medicine"; here meaning a restorative or beneficial health treatment.</note>.

If the account I have given is plausible in itself and free from absurdity, and if it clearly conveys the idea I intended to the reader’s mind, then it does not matter at all whether such a school ever actually existed. That does not fall within my claim; it is sufficient for my purpose if such a school *could* have existed. Perhaps another reader will devise an easier, clearer, and more probable method of interpreting this inscription. If so, I shall not argue with him or envy him for his creative imagination <note>original: "Fertility of his Invention"</note>. My account of the matter serves to support the theory I have formed regarding the possibility of the thing, and therefore

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of his hypothesis that the breath of young people, specifically young girls, can prolong life. He argues that even if his specific scenario is a thought experiment, the underlying principle of the restorative power of breath remains valid.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>

Just as a mathematician draws his figures in order to explain the propositions he presents, so I have displayed this example to illustrate the theory <note>original: "Doctrine"</note> that I would recommend.

One might also suggest that most of the examples cited so far regarding the long lives of those who have taught young people do not quite reach the point I have proposed, since they all admittedly involve young men. Yet, even if this objection affects what I have delivered, the credibility of the inscription remains untouched, as some understand it to refer to boys, though I incline to interpret it as referring to girls for reasons that will appear later. 

But whoever makes this objection must admit, at the time of making it, that there is an extraordinary effectiveness <note>original: "Efficacy"</note> in the breath of young men. When duly considered, this will be found to be a very great concession in my favor; for if there is any such effectiveness in the breath of young men, no reason can be assigned why my hypothesis should be considered less reasonable. Everything, therefore, that generally supports the benefits of human breath, or specifically the breath of young people, tends to strengthen and support what I have laid down. 

As to those particular circumstances which render female breath preferable, it is my responsibility to explain them, and this I shall do in its proper place. In the meantime, allow me to remark on one thing more in favor of my hypothetical scenario <note>original: "fictitious History"; here meaning a constructed or imagined narrative used for illustration.</note>.

of the College of Virgins, namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, that the greatest men have thought it acceptable <note>original: "allowable"</note> to introduce such descriptions wherever they have judged them necessary <note>original: "requisite"</note>, or appropriate to the subject they were treating. "If," says that learned person, "your system is founded on truth—if there really is any such vigor and power <note>original: "efficacy"</note> in the breath of women, or even of young women—how does it happen that those men who are known to have had the greatest possible advantages in this way have not reached an extraordinary age?" This is the first objection, and it must be admitted that Mr. Nunning has expressed it very modestly, for Solomon had many other advantages besides this. He was undoubtedly a great philosopher and an excellent naturalist who perfectly understood the art of managing <note>original: "conducting"</note> life. We imagine our old man to have a gentle, consistent, and pleasant disposition—always at ease, always content, and surrounded by many blooming virgins who are too young to have either their bodies or their thoughts corrupted <note>original: "tainted"</note>. But the life of *Solomon* was the very opposite of this. His thoughts were in a perpetual hurry and his mind in constant agitation <note>original: "Adgitation"</note>. On the third day, he gave free rein <note>original: "giving a loose"</note> to his pleasures, abandoning himself without restraint not only to the lustfulness <note>original: "Lewdness"</note> of his own desires, but to the wilder excesses of a multitude of immoral <note>original: "vicious"</note> women.

Is there any similarity between these two characters? Can one imagine that the company <note>original: "Conversation"</note> of women in such different circumstances would have the same effect on *Solomon* and *Hermippus*? What I have said will, I trust, satisfy the honorable gentleman who raised this doubt, and every reader who considers it seriously. The objection assumes that Solomon was in the same situation in which I would place Hermippus, but I have shown this to be completely without merit. <margin>ᵍ 1 Kings 11:4</margin>
All the accounts we have of *Solomon* assume him to be under a special divine plan <note>original: "Dispensation"</note>, and show that he had direct access to God himself at least twice in his life. Therefore, this man was not in the same situation as others and is not fit to be considered an example for my argument. I might take this even further by observing that God was pleased to promise him a long life <note>original: "length of Days"</note> if he imitated the righteousness of his father *David*. Thus, he had a much better remedy <note>original: "Medicine"</note> in his power than any human wisdom could provide him, and yet he did not know how to use it. The Scriptures say that when *Solomon* was old, his wives turned away his heart after other *gods*ᵍ. "Oldness" is used here in the sense that I understand it—that is, regarding his physical and mental condition, and not the actual years of his life. In terms of years, *Solomon* was not truly old when he died, since he did not exceed sixty-seven. The plain meaning of this text, therefore, must be that when his vices had broken his health <note>original: "Constitution"</note> and weakened his mental abilities <note>original: "Faculties"</note>, then these women to whom he had surrendered himself led him into idolatry. These "nurses" of his second childhood diligently led him to his downfall.

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that King Solomon's short life was a result of his own choices, despite divine promises, and begins a new comparison between the healthy lifestyles of ancient Europeans and the decadence of Asian royalty.</meta>

Drawn by their delusions, he sacrificed both claims <note>original: "Titles"</note> to a long life: namely, that which temperance <note>original: "Temperance"; here meaning moderation in diet and lifestyle</note> gave him by the law of nature, and that which he received from God himself through an extraordinary revelation. For the latter undoubtedly included the former, since we cannot suppose that God promised long life to *Solomon* without an implied condition—that he attend to the natural means for attaining it. The laws and promises of God must always be rational, because they are made and proposed to reasonable creatures.

After examining this objection so fully, it will take me much less trouble to clear up the other. For what can there be more contrary to, or irreconcilable with, the doctrine I have laid down than the lives led by *Eastern Monarchs*? All writers agree that to give strength and firmness to the body, nothing is as necessary as chastity in one's youth <note>original: "green Years"</note>. The *Germans*, as *Tacitus* tells us, were accustomed to refrain from women until they were at least thirty, and he identifies this as the primary cause of their robustness, courage, and long life <sup>h</sup>. Long before his time, the *Spartans* had formed the same ideas, led to them by reason and experience <sup>i</sup>. <meta>This page continues the argument that ancient physicians recognized the restorative power of youthful contact, transitioning from the biblical example of King David to the writings of the medieval philosopher Roger Bacon.</meta>

...as it lies in the Bible <note>original: "the Book"</note>, and from this I think it plainly appears that the physicians of those times, who were well acquainted with the effectiveness <note>original: "Efficacy"</note> of this remedy, understood how the elderly might be benefited by youth.

The famous monk **Roger Bacon**, whom I have cited before, has a large chapter on this subject in his treatise *On the Cure of Old Age*. Although he wrote to a Pope in an age that was not at all favorable to him or his discoveries, he thought it proper to conceal what he admits to be the most effective medicine he could prescribe. He hid it under such dark and obscure terms that I believe few have understood his true meaning. It may perhaps be suspected that I have deceived myself, and that having this notion in my head, I have found things in **Bacon’s**¹ works that were never there. For that reason, and because I can offer nothing more interesting <note>original: "curious"</note> or more relevant of my own, I will cite as much of his book as relates to this subject and leave it to my reader to decide how far I do or do not accurately represent him <note>original: "do him Justice"</note> by my commentary.

> “I have read many volumes written by the wise, and I find few things in medicine <note>original: "Physic"</note> which restore the **natural heat** <term>natural heat</term> <note>In ancient medicine, "natural heat" was the vital internal fire thought to sustain life and digestion, which naturally diminished as a person aged.</note>, which is weakened by the loss <note>original: "Dissolution"</note> of the **innate moisture** <term>innate moisture</term> <note>In Galenic medicine, this was the "oil" that fueled the natural heat; its depletion was considered the primary cause of aging.</note>, or the increase of a foreign moisture.”

Regarding its nature <note>original: "Disposition"</note>, they say it is like youth itself and possesses a balanced and moderate **temperament** <term>complexion</term> <note>In ancient medicine, a "complexion" was the specific balance of the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—within a person.</note>.

“The signs of a balanced temperament in men are: when their skin color is composed of white and red <note>a healthy, ruddy glow</note>, and when their hair is yellow, leaning toward redness and curls. According to ***Pliny*** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a famous Roman naturalist and author</note>, these signs also include: when the flesh is moderate in both quality and quantity; when a man’s dreams are delightful; when his facial expression <note>original: "Countenance"</note> is cheerful and pleasant; and when he is moderate in his appetite for eating and drinking.

“This medicine is indeed like such a temperament, for it has a moderate heat. Its aroma <note>original: "Fume"</note> is mild and sweet, and pleasing to the smell. When it loses this balanced temperature, it loses its power and goodness. Therefore, this medicine provides moderate heat because it is moderately hot; it heals because it is healthy <note>original: "whole"</note>. When it is sick, it makes a man sick. When it is diseased <note>original: "Distempered"</note>, it causes diseases and changes the body to match its own condition, because of the similarity <note>original: "similitude"</note> it shares with the body.”

" For the illness <note>original: "Infirmity"</note> of a wild animal rarely passes into a human, but rather into another animal of the same species. " For **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD) was a Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a thousand years.</note> says that whatever is released from any thing must necessarily be absorbed <note>original: "assimilated"</note> by that thing. This is clearly seen in how diseases pass from one person to another, such as failing eyesight <note>original: "Weakness of the Eyes"</note> or infectious <note>original: "Pestilential"</note> diseases.

" This substance has a remarkable quality: it not only protects human bodies from decay <note>original: "Corruption"</note>, but it also guards plants against rotting <note>original: "Putrefaction"</note>. This substance is rarely found, and even when it is found, it cannot be easily <note>original: "commodiously"</note> obtained by everyone.

" Instead, wise men use the medicine that is found deep within the earth—already complete and prepared—as well as those found in the sea, and that which is the 'square stone' of the **noble animal** <term>noble animal</term> <note>The "noble animal" is a common alchemical code for a human being.</note>. This is done so that every part may be protected from the contamination <note>original: "Infection"</note> of another. But if that 'stone' cannot be obtained, other elements should be used after they have been separated, divided, and purified.

“where he says that there is a certain medicine concealed by wise men, lest the unprincipled <note>original: "incontinent"; in this context, it refers to those lacking moral self-restraint or the spiritually unworthy.</note> should offend their Creator. There is such a heat in this thing as exists in young men of a healthy constitution <term>complexion</term> <note>In pre-modern medicine, 'complexion' refers to the balance of the four bodily humors.</note>, and if I dared to declare the properties of this heat, this most hidden secret would immediately be revealed. For this heat helps those with paralysis <note>original: "the Palsical"</note>; it restores and preserves the wasted strength of the natural heat <note>original: "Native Heat"; the innate warmth believed to be the source of life and digestion.</note>, causes it to flourish in all the limbs <note>original: "Members"</note>, and gently revives the elderly.”

These are precisely the words of *Friar Bacon*; the matter now to be considered is whether we have correctly interpreted them, or whether they are capable of a more accurate and natural meaning. After a few reflections on the reasons that have guided us, this shall be left to the reader’s judgment.

Some have held the opinion that this mysterious preparation was nothing other than the quintessence <term>quintessence</term> <note>In alchemy, the 'fifth essence' or the purest, most concentrated medicinal force extracted from a substance.</note> of human blood. However, whoever considers the description of it in all its parts will easily see that it can be no such thing, since its odor or scent is recommended because of its pleasing sweetness. Furthermore, quintessences are typically taken internally, whereas he directs the medicine to be applied as a plaster <note>original: "Plaister"; a medicated bandage or substance applied to the skin.</note> to the stomach; indeed, the other characteristics agree with a blood-based quintessence as poorly as this one does. These are precisely the words of Friar *Bacon*; the thing now to be considered is whether we have rightly interpreted them, or whether they are capable of any more accurate and more natural sense, which—after a few reflections on the motives that have guided us—shall be left to the reader’s judgment.
