--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding Roger Bacon’s writing style, specifically addressing why he used such cryptic language to describe his secret medicine.</meta>

[ 62 ]

...that our author intended to describe a kind of precious stone here, but anyone who thinks so must be completely unfamiliar with his style of writing. He is not someone who uses mysteries just to shock his readers or to build his own reputation; he rightfully despised both of those behaviors. His obscurity comes from the fact that, at the time he wrote, he was in prison for writing against the popular philosophy of the day. He addressed this book to his prosecutor, Pope Nicholas IV, in order to seek a pardon. However, because he was unsure how effective his appeal would be, he was very cautious in his writing. He wanted to avoid giving cause for a second accusation—specifically, the charge of revealing secrets that should remain hidden from the general public <term>vulgar capacities</term> <note>original: "vulgar capacities"; this refers to the common people or those lacking the specialized education to handle "dangerous" or high-level knowledge.</note>.

Now, if he were actually describing a precious stone, why would he speak so obscurely? Describing a stone certainly would not have required such great efforts to hide the meaning. In fact, such a vague account would have worked against him; it would have invited wild speculation in a situation where he could have spoken plainly without any danger at all. On the other hand, if we accept our interpretation <note>The commentator argues that the "medicine" is not a literal substance but a metaphor or a more complex alchemical process.</note>, there was every reason for such caution. His apology <term>apology</term> <note>In this context, an "apology" is a formal defense of one's positions or actions, rather than an expression of regret.</note> then becomes perfectly logical, and all the doubts and hesitations he included seem like clever and appropriately placed modest excuses.

<meta>catchword: LET</meta>

<summary>The author argues that Roger Bacon's obscure writing style was a necessary defense mechanism to avoid further persecution while in prison, rather than an attempt to describe a literal gemstone.</summary>
<keywords>Roger Bacon, Pope Nicholas IV, secret medicine, alchemical metaphors, persecution, censorship, 13th-century philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the interpretation of Roger Bacon's "medicine," arguing that his cryptic language is actually an allegorical reference to the restorative power of youth, drawing a parallel to the biblical imagery found in the Song of Solomon.</meta>

[ 63 ]

LET us consider this description by Bacon and compare it with the picture drawn by the hand of a greater and even more knowledgeable man; we will likely think we are hearing Solomon describing the lovely Shunamite <note>original: "Shunamite"; while the text uses this term, it likely conflates the "Shulamite" bride of the Song of Solomon with Abishag the "Shunammite," a young woman brought to King David in his old age to restore his warmth and vitality.</note>. Look at Bacon’s words again and imagine the thin veil being removed; you will then see the perfect figure of this young woman who was very beautiful <term>fair</term>. You will perceive the Rose of Sharon and the Lily of Damascus; her hair like purple in curling locks; her two young fawns <term>roes</term> that are twins, feeding among the lilies; her head filled with the dew and her locks with the drops of the night; her face <term>countenance</term> as beautiful as the moon and as bright as the sun; her fruit sweet to the taste as she sits <note>original: "sets"</note> under the shade with great delight; her spikenard <term>spikenard</term> <note>An expensive, aromatic ointment or perfume derived from an ancient plant, mentioned frequently in the Bible.</note> and finest spices, while the south wind blows upon her garden so that its scents <note>original: "spices"</note> may spread; her well of living waters and streams from Lebanon. And here, the most beautiful of women is wounded by the watchmen, and then her beloved departs.

THIS commentary sufficiently explains the text, but to remove any doubt and to apply all this effectively to my subject, I shall briefly discuss two or three remarkable passages. In the first place, our author says that this medicine is compared to that which comes out of the "mine of the noble animal," and what can this "mine" be but a woman? Within her, as in a mine, the noble animal <term>noble animal</term> <note>In the philosophical tradition of the 17th century, the "noble animal" refers to a human being, considered the most refined of all living creatures.</note>

<summary>The author argues that Roger Bacon’s mysterious "medicine" is actually an allegory for the restorative power of young women, comparing Bacon’s descriptions to the erotic and botanical imagery in Solomon’s Song of Songs. He suggests that the "mine" mentioned in Bacon's work is a metaphor for the female body as the source of life and vitality.</summary>
<keywords>Roger Bacon, Solomon, Song of Solomon, Shunamite, Shulamite, allegory, alchemy, medicine, noble animal, Rose of Sharon, gerocomy</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the interpretation of Roger Bacon's ideas, specifically focusing on the biological and spiritual connection between humans and the restorative power of youth, referencing the biblical story of King David and Abishag.</meta>

[ 64 ]

[ani]mal is formed. Then again, our author says that the sickness <note>original: "Infirmity"</note> of a brute animal rarely passes into a human, but rather into another animal of the same kind; however, the sickness of one human passes into another, and so does health, because of their similarity. Here he reveals a great secret in philosophy: namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, that there is a "sympathy" in health just as there is contagion in disease. Just as a diseased breath infects, a healthy and wholesome one may refresh and energize <note>original: "exhilarate"</note>. Taken in this sense, nothing can be easier, plainer, or more rational; nor is there any other meaning that the words, taken literally, can bear. 

The last passage I shall remark upon is when he observes that *Venus* <term>Venus</term> <note>original: "Venus"; here the author uses the name of the goddess of love as a metaphor for sexual activity or intercourse.</note> weakens and diminishes the power and virtue of this remedy. How exactly does this agree with the relevant reflection of the author of the Book of *Kings*? When repeating the uses *David* made of *Abishag*, he says with particular emphasis: *but the king knew her not* <note>A reference to 1 Kings 1:4, indicating that while Abishag provided the elderly King David with physical warmth, they did not have sexual relations.</note>. A young virgin taken in this manner recalls heat and life into an old man; but if he forgets that this is a temporary and artificial vigor and uses it as if it were a natural one, he may indeed give life to another, but it will be at the expense of his own. When these exploits are described to us as marks of an old man’s vigor, we should record them as marks of his folly as well; for as *Solomon* says, *there is a time to be born, and a time to die*.

<summary>The author explains Roger Bacon's theory that health can be transmitted between humans through proximity, warning that sexual activity (Venus) depletes the life-extending benefits of this youth-based "remedy."</summary>
<keywords>Roger Bacon, David and Abishag, contagion, sympathy of health, longevity, Venus, King David, Solomon</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of the theory that proximity to youth can prolong life. It addresses objections regarding the lifespans of Eastern rulers, arguing that their longevity—despite high-stress lives—proves the effectiveness of this "remedy."</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<sig>I</sig>

So Nature and true philosophy teach that there is a time to father children <note>original: "beget"</note>, and a time to abstain from fathering children.

I will also take the liberty of drawing some advantage even from the second objection. Regarding Eastern princes, while it is true that most of them die young, it is equally true that when they do not die violent deaths, they frequently live to a very old age—for example, *Shah Abbas* <note>Shah Abbas I (1571–1629), the Safavid Shah of Persia.</note> and *Aurangzeb* <note>original: "Aurengzebe"; Aurangzeb (1618–1707), the sixth Mughal Emperor.</note>. Now, consider the immense fatigue these monarchs endured: the many battles they fought, the tedious marches they made, the variety of dangers they faced, and the vast depletion of energy <note>original: "Expence of Spirits"; in this period, "animal spirits" were thought to be the fluid or energy that powered the body's functions.</note> that a life of such constant action and intense thought must cause. In a country where the art of *medicine* <note>original: "Physic"</note> is far from being as advanced as it is even in *Europe*, it would be hard to find in such places any medicine capable of producing such an extraordinary effect, if we exclude this remedy of which I am speaking. 

For although I am very doubtful—and therefore place no emphasis on—the healthfulness of the breath of women, yet in the *harems* <term>Seraglios</term> <note>original: "Seraglios"; the secluded living quarters of women in an Ottoman or Persian palace.</note> of such princes, there are always so many young virgins who have such close access to the person of the prince. I cannot help but believe that his vigor and health are greatly supported by these "fragrant streams" <note>original: "odoriferous Streams"; a reference to the theory that youth exude a restorative aura or "breath" that can be absorbed by the elderly.</note>.

I cannot help adding what seems to be an even stronger argument in favor of my hypothesis: that the *Arab* princes scattered through the dominions of the Ottoman Sultan <note>original: "Grand Signior"; the title commonly used in Europe for the Sultan of the Ottoman Empire.</note>, who are less exposed to foreign wars or domestic intrigues than other Eastern princes, actually live beyond the ordinary age of man. They die at last as every man ought to die—simply because their blood can no longer circulate <note>original: "run no longer"</note>. At the very least, I have a right to use a circumstance as probable as this to support my opinion, especially when it is thought reasonable to take a fact—which has only a superficial resemblance to the case I have stated—as a sufficient ground for an objection against the position I am trying to prove. Probability is certainly more closely related to evidence than a mere similarity of circumstances, which vanishes upon a strict examination.

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of how physiological processes—specifically the circulation of the blood and "insensible perspiration"—allow the qualities of one body to be transferred to another through the air.</meta>

<page-num>[ 67 ]</page-num>
<sig>I 2</sig>

[...the] skill and learning of that incomparable *Englishman, Doctor Harvey* <gloss>m</gloss>; [it is established] that a great part of our nourishment <note>original: "Aliments"</note>, after entering the blood, is expelled from the body in such an unnoticeable way that this kind of evacuation is very properly called "insensible perspiration." This was first carefully examined by *Sanctorius*, who found that this process alone was far more significant than all the other bodily secretions combined <gloss>n</gloss>. If this is true—and no person in their right mind now doubts that it is—then it follows that there must be massive quantities of the matter from this insensible perspiration mixed into the air surrounding the perspiring bodies. Consequently, this air must be strongly saturated <note>original: "impregnated"</note> with the qualities inherent to that matter.

If we push this inquiry still further and work to acquaint ourselves with the nature of the particles that are thrown off in this way, a little

---

<gloss>m</gloss> Our author alludes here to the remarkable success <note>original: "Felicity"</note> of Doctor Harvey, who first discovered and then fully demonstrated the theory <note>original: "Doctrine"</note> of the circulation of the blood. At first, it was opposed with great anger and bitterness <note>original: "Heat and Spleen"</note>—as most new inventions are—but it came at last to be as generally accepted and admired as it deserves. Doctor Harvey died in 1657, thirty years after he first published his discovery in his *Anatomical Exercise on the Motion of the Heart and Blood in Animals* <note>original: "Exercitatio Anatomica de motu cordis"</note>, printed at Frankfurt in 1627.

<gloss>n</gloss> See his famous treatise *On Static Medicine* <note>original: "de Medicina Statica"</note>, which the English reader may consult in his own language, translated by the wise and tireless Dr. *Quincy*.

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s physiological explanation of how the "vital breath" or "balsamic steam" of young people can be physically transferred to an older person nearby through the air they share.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>

A little attention will make this matter plain. For since insensible perspiration <note>The process of moisture and gases leaving the body through the skin and lungs without being noticed as sweat.</note> is caused by the circulation of the blood, it necessarily follows that the particles thrown off by the blood must share the nature of the fluid from which they are expelled. Now we know the blood of young people to be soft, oily, and restorative <note>original: "balsamic"</note>, as well as we can know anything, because this is evident to us from its effects: a brisk and lively disposition, perfect health, and quick growth. For all of these, a blood composed in this way is absolutely necessary. Based on these principles, it is clear that the matter perspired by young people—in the circumstances I have assumed the pupils of *Hermippus* to be in—must have all the qualities of their blood. It must be a soft, smooth, slippery, and restorative <note>original: "balsamic"</note> vapor <note>original: "steam"</note> continually flowing from their bodies. Since they are constantly drawing in the surrounding <note>original: "circumambient"</note> air and continually throwing off this matter, they must, in the span of a few hours, absolutely change the entire volume of air in a room where they are taught by their teacher <note>original: "preceptor"</note>. In that case, the teacher will receive into himself a large proportion of this perspirable matter, in the same way that it is thrown off by them.

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of the theory that health can be transferred through the air. It transitions from the previous page's discussion of "whimsical" ideas to a scientific justification based on the experiments of Sanctorius.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>

whimsical and chimerical <note>The word "whimsical" begins on the previous page.</note>, and for this very reason, I have not insisted upon it as extensively as I might have, or in such strong and direct terms. I might have entered into great detail regarding the calculations <note>original: "Computations"</note> that have been made about the quantity of matter thrown off in this way, and from those I might have deduced many curious and—to competent judges—convincing proofs. However, I do not care for this overly academic <note>original: "critical"</note> style of writing, which might possibly lose many of my readers and secure the agreement only of those who are least likely to put this doctrine into practice. 

But allow me to observe that what I have laid down is an indisputable fact, and not one of those topics about which much has been written but nothing settled. *Sanctorius* <note>Santorio Santorio (1561–1636), an Italian physician who famously weighed himself before and after eating to study metabolism.</note>, who first treated this matter sensibly and practically, had the honor of perfecting his discovery and taking the subject as far as it could go. He did this using the best method: that of experimentation. He had a chair fixed on a scale <note>original: "Ballance"</note> and fitted with springs in such a way that it could detect the slightest change in his weight. By using this chair, and by constant observation of what he ate, drank, perspired, and excreted <note>original: "Evacuated"</note> in every other way, he gained certain knowledge of what he has shared. Therefore, we may believe with the utmost confidence that at least one-half of what we eat or drink—after passing through

<page-num>74</page-num>

<margin>
q See his *Chemistry*, vol. i. p. 151, where his words are translated thus: "The most subtle part of the juices of animals is a fine spirit, which is continually exhaling, in which the specific character of the animal seems to reside, and by which it is distinguished from all others. We may infer this from hounds, which, across a long stretch of ground and through a multitude of crossing paths, will distinguish a particular animal out of a whole flock, having recently scented the effluvia <term>invisible particles or vapors</term> of its footsteps; or they will find their master through a hundred different paths in the middle of a confused crowd of people. From this, we may infer how thin and subtle, yet how different from all other kinds of matter, these effluvia must be. They seem to be of an oily origin, or to exist in a subtle, oily medium, as may appear both from the analogy of things and other properties."

...our reason, if they fall under any one of them. For instance, if I plainly discern the smell of a rose at a certain distance, my reason will tell me that I am within the "atmosphere" of that flower. It is impossible that I should discern its odor by smell if its effluvia <term>effluvia: invisible particles or vapors emitted by a substance</term> did not strike the proper organ of that sense in me. Therefore, with a little reflection, I can easily form a concept of this flower "perspiring" <note>original: "perspiring"; used here in the sense of breathing out or emitting particles</note> an infinite number of odor-bearing particles. For a certain distance, these particles overcome all the other different types of matter floating in the air enough to be detected by my sense of smell*.

But now, if we take this the other way and consider a body continually emitting matter that is "insensible" to us—that is, particles so subtle <note>original: "subtile"</note> that they escape the notice of all our sensory organs—we would be extremely slow to understand if we did not realize that this emitting body must have its own atmosphere, just like the rose or any other fragrant flower. It is, therefore, I think, a point established beyond all dispute, that if a number of young women <note>original: "Virgins"</note> are in the company of an old man, he must draw from them into himself a great quantity of that subtle matter, the qualities of which have been described before.

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 75, which established that all bodies possess an "atmosphere" of invisible particles (effluvia). The author now moves from the idea of bodies emitting these particles to the idea that the skin can also absorb them.</meta>

...and especially if we consider that as these effluvia <term>effluvia: invisible particles or vapors emitted by a substance</term> escape through the pores, the pores must consequently be continually open. If this is so, they must absorb from the outside as well as allow passage to what comes from within.

I suspect this will appear as a new paradox <term>paradox: a statement that seems contradictory or absurd but may be true</term> to many of my readers. I can almost see some of them ready to throw my treatise <note>original: "Treatise"; a formal written work on a subject</note> out of their hands, crying with an air of self-importance and disdain: "This poor man is insane, and would gladly make us so too!" But have a little patience; allow me only to propose a few familiar questions, and I will defy you to disbelieve what I have stated. 

Is there anything more common than to find a strangury <term>strangury: a condition causing painful, frequent, and difficult urination</term> follows the application of a blistering plaster? Yet how can this happen if the particles of the cantharides <note>original: "Cantharides"; also known as "Spanish fly," these beetles were dried and used in medicine to cause blistering on the skin</note> did not enter through the pores? By entering, they create a disturbance in that salty, watery fluid which is usually secreted by the kidneys and makes up what we call urine. 

On the other hand, it is certain that opium used in a medical plaster <note>original: "Plaister"</note> will induce sleep. It could not possibly do this if it did not similarly find a passage through the pores into the blood. I might also point out an effect that frequently—if not constantly—results from the application of a cataplasm <term>cataplasm: a poultice or soft, moist mass of herbs applied to the body</term> of chamomile to the stomach: the bitter taste of that herb can be detected on the palate within two hours. I will mention one more instance,

This author tells us<sup>t</sup> that in the year 1346, there emerged from the earth in Cathay <note>original: "Cathay"; a historical name for Northern China</note> (which is that part of Great Tartary <note>original: "Great Tartary"; a historical region in North and Central Asia</note> bordering China) a certain vapor so incredibly foul-smelling that it destroyed all living creatures. From Cathay, it passed through Asia and Greece; from there it crossed into Africa, and after ravaging that country, it entered Europe in 1348, causing such [havoc] <note>The catchword "Havock" indicates the next page begins with this word, describing the devastation of the Black Death.</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's account of a devastating plague (the Black Death) that originated as a subterranean "vapor" in Asia. <page-num>78</page-num>

Havoc in *France*, so that not even a city, village, or single house escaped; from there, it passed into other countries, reaching even the furthest extremities of the North. The venom, according to my author, was so contagious that it could infect someone just by sight. It was noted that it lasted exactly five months in every country through which it passed. In those places where it was most mild <note>original: "favourable"</note>, it left only a third of the inhabitants alive; in most, it left about a fifteenth; and in some, no more than a twentieth part remained. Can one imagine that an exhalation <term>exhalation: a vapor, gas, or smell given off by the earth or atmosphere, believed in this period to influence health</term> should travel all the way around the globe and produce such terrible effects wherever it went, yet believe that the vapors constantly surrounding us have no effect at all? Or should we fear that if such dreadful feats can be performed by stinking and harmful exhalations, there is nothing nourishing or healthy to be achieved by those of an opposite kind? I must confess that I see no reason for such a belief.

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the "Hermippus" method, where the proximity of young people might rejuvenate the elderly through the exchange of "vapors" or "exhalations." He is now connecting this theory to the mechanical medical theories of the 18th century, specifically those of Hermann Boerhaave.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>

<margin>Hermann Boerhaave, *Medical Institutes* <note>original: *Instit. Medic.*</note>, sections 1053, 1054.</margin>
...have laid down. Reason and experience have convinced us that the body is a pneumatic-hydraulic machine <term>pneumatic-hydraulic machine: a 17th- and 18th-century medical concept viewing the body as a mechanical system of pipes and valves operated by the movement of air (or "animal spirits") and liquids</term>, composed of fluids and solids. A good constitution, or a healthy disposition, arises from the proper motion of the solids and a correct circulation of the fluids.

It is also no less certain that this motion and this circulation depend reciprocally on each other. The circulation is obstructed, lessened, and in some measure stopped by a lack of proper motion in the solids, caused by their loss of the proper tone and texture <term>tone and texture: the healthy elasticity and physical structure of body tissues</term> they ought to have. On the other hand, this very loss arises from the solids not receiving a timely and suitable supply of nutrition from the circulating fluids <note>original: "Juices"</note>.

Hence arises that dryness, stiffness, and rigidity of the fibers which specifically causes the disease we call "old age." To prevent this in a natural, rational, and physical way, the only proper method is to provide a constant, equal, and effective supply of smooth, balsamic <term>balsamic: healing, soothing, or oily in nature</term>, and lubricating particles from the circulating fluids. If this could once be achieved, it seems very apparent that old age could no more attack the human body than any other disease against which proper precautions may be taken. But, as it is not to be expected that human wisdom should be able to perfect such a method...

<summary>The author explains the mechanical view of the body as a system of fluids and solids, arguing that "old age" is actually a disease caused by the fibers becoming stiff and dry when they lack proper nutrition from the blood.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the physical decline of old age can be delayed by replenishing the body's solids with fine particles derived from healthy sources—specifically the breath and exhalations of the young.</meta>

<page-num>[ 81 ]</page-num>

...method, all that we can reasonably hope for is to arrive at some proficiency in it; so that although we are not able to prevent old age, we may at least have it in our power to delay it. It is, in a way, incredible that by art <term>art: medical skill or human intervention</term> a man should be able to reach two or three hundred years; but that he should live and enjoy life to over a hundred, the wisest of the ancients believed. The methods they prescribed and practiced for this purpose are all founded upon my principles, as appears from their use of *frictions*, *baths*, and *ointments*. All of these would have been pointless and improper if they had not believed it possible to charge the bodily fluids, by means of them, with such particles as were fit to repair the losses of the solids <note>In 18th-century "mechanical" medicine, the "solids" (fibers and tissues) were thought to wear down and required replenishment from "particles" in the "fluids" (blood and lymph).</note>.

HERMIPPUS <note>Hermippus Redivivus: a legendary figure who supposedly lived to age 115 by being constantly surrounded by young women.</note> undoubtedly made use of a far more rational as well as effective medicine. For we have proved, by all the methods that the nature of our argument will allow, that the particles breathed <term>respired: inhaled and exhaled</term> and released <term>perspired: emitted through the skin</term> by young persons in full health are the smoothest, softest, and most nutritious that can be imagined. He inhaled these particles, but he also drew them in through all the pores of his body. This kind of "animal action" <term>animal action: involuntary biological processes, such as absorption or circulation</term>, which is largely spontaneous, must have increased their effects. Taking all of this together, one cannot help but view him as a person in a constant warm bath of the most refined and oily fluids <note>original: "spirituous and unctuous Humours"</note>. These were continually soaking into him, while at the same time, through the vapors of the young people’s breath, he drank in life itself rather than just air. 

If we add one further detail—one that is not at all forced or improbable—namely, that he had some of these young people lie with him, just as King David did with Abishag <note>A reference to 1 Kings 1:1–4, where the elderly King David was kept warm by a young virgin named Abishag to restore his health.</note>, then we take this "medicine" to its highest level. We can easily understand that during those times of free and abundant perspiration, he must have received such a large supply of "nutritive spirits" <term>nutritive spirits: vital energies believed to be carried in bodily fluids and breath to sustain the body's tissues</term> that it effectively refreshed his physical nature. This would have restored, in large part, the wear and tear caused by the ordinary functions of life.

We can add the greatest weight to this line of reasoning if we are permitted to argue from opposites. What is more common

...than to see a woman advanced in years become not only brisk and lively, but strong and healthy, by marrying a young husband. She drinks his breath, breathes out <note>original: "exhales"</note> his spirits, extracts his moisture, and thereby invigorates herself. Meanwhile, the poor man suffers from the impure contagion of her breath and vapors; and from the harmfulness <note>original: "Malignity"</note> of this ill-chosen union, he sinks very quickly into apparent weakness and falls at last into what the common people call a **galloping consumption** <term>galloping consumption: a historical term for a rapidly progressing form of tuberculosis or any disease causing rapid wasting of the body</term>. 

How strange! [He] fell into a very extraordinary and dangerous illness <note>original: "Distemper"</note>; he eventually grew so weak and frail that his case was judged to be hopeless. Gradually, however, he grew better, and as his strength increased, his gray hair and white beard fell off bit by bit, and his skin also began to peel. Soon after, light, strong hair began to emerge <note>original: "peep"</note> through the skin of his head, which in time grew down to his shoulders in strong, natural curls. His beard grew back in the same way, and his face acquired a healthy, glowing <note>original: "florid"</note> complexion. In a word, he became again a healthy, vigorous <note>original: "lusty"</note> and—if the expression may be allowed—a young man. His wife was forced to testify <note>original: "attest"</note> to this, as she later had several fine boys by him<sup>a</sup>. This is an instance very relevant to my purpose, since it proves the extraordinary power <note>original: "prodigious Efficacy"</note> of human breath and the substances exhaled <note>original: "perspired"</note> by a youthful <note>original: "juveniel"</note> body. Yet, allow me to observe that this extraordinary effort of nature might possibly have contributed to shortening the old man's life. Without the enjoyment of this young woman, he might have preserved his health <note>original: "Constitution"</note> for many years and lived in the perfect enjoyment <note>original: "Fruition"</note> of those pleasures specific to the mind—the very enjoyment for which it seems most rational that life should be prolonged.

There is something of the same kind observable in the well-known history of the famous *English long-liver, Thomas Parr*. These details <note>original: "particulars"</note> are very carefully <note>original: "curiously"</note> noted by the great anatomist *Bartholin*, who recorded his account of him not as a curiosity to please children, but as a singular and memorable fact worthy of the attention and consideration of men. 

This *Parr* was born at *Winnington*, in the county of *Salop* <note>Shropshire</note>, in 1483. He spent his youth in very hard labor and—what is just as remarkable—in sobriety and chastity. At eighty <note>original: "fourscore"</note> years old, he married his first wife, *Jane*, with whom he had two children. Neither of them were long-lived or showed any extraordinary signs of strength; the first died at the age of one month, and the second lived only a few years. 

At 102, he became enamored with *Katherine Milton*, whom he got with child; he performed public penance in the church for it. Some months before he died, the *Earl of Arundel* brought him up to *London* and presented him to *King Charles I*. However, due to the change of air and lifestyle <note>original: "Living"</note>, he died soon after. It was believed he might have survived many more years if he had remained in his own part of the country and led the same life he was accustomed to ᵇ. This man was overgrown with

<meta>This page continues the author's argument for the scientific pursuit of longevity, picking up from the previous discussion on the possibility of rejuvenating the human body. ...remember upon this occasion what I have already cited from the learned monk *Bacon* <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292), a Franciscan friar and philosopher who early on advocated for the scientific method and wrote on the "Cure of Old Age."</note>. What does he say? If *Aristotle*, *Plato*, *Hippocrates*, and *Galen* <note>These represent the pinnacle of ancient Greek philosophy and medicine.</note> were ignorant of this secret, is that proof that we shall not attain it? Were they not ignorant of many other secrets that are now commonly known? Why, then, should we imagine the barrier of science is fixed here, rather than anywhere else? 

Why should we not find out the means of prolonging life, just as we seek a method for squaring the circle <note>squaring the circle: a famous mathematical problem that, for centuries, represented the quest for a solution to a seemingly impossible task.</note>? Is not the former of as great consequence to us as human beings? Or is the latter a more useful proof of the strength of human understanding? 

Let us proceed then; let us collect and compare—since the nature of the subject forbids other experiments—such examples as we meet with in authentic histories. Let us in this, as in other cases, endeavor to convert history into science by observing closely the details in every account <note>original: "Relation"</note> and endeavoring thereby to trace out the manner of nature’s working; for if this can be once done, we shall soon be able to follow her steps. If nature at any time grants <note>original: "vouchsafes"</note> this favor to men, it follows that their bodies have no incapacity of receiving it. That is to say, if every man’s body was not so constituted as to exceed by far the ordinary limits of life, it could not possibly happen that any man’s life should be so extended.

Another <note>The text begins mid-word; "another" completes the word "Ano-ther" from the previous page.</note> circumstance which I must mention, as it strongly supports my theory, is that this man—who lived to a greater age than any other for whom we have reliable evidence—had many wives. These, I say, are points that deserve consideration. If such a power exists within the human constitution, we should not lose hope of finding methods that might help it manifest more frequently. This would certainly be a far greater benefit to humanity than simply studying the proper remedies for usual and common illnesses <note>original: "distempers"</note>. Let me add that perhaps, if such a method could be found, it would also protect against almost all diseases by creating such vigor that it would not allow them to take hold <note>original: "not permit the entrance of them"</note>.

Cardinal *Richelieu* <note>original: "Richlieu"</note> would have collapsed under the weight of his political duties if he had not been relieved from time to time by the humorous buffoonery of *Boisrobert* <note>François le Métel de Boisrobert (1592–1662), a French poet and priest known for his wit</note>. *Louis XIV* <note>original: "Lewis XIV"</note> was as refined in his amusements as he was great in his councils; he showed as much sound judgment in approving a play by *Molière* as he did in approving a military project by *Louvois* <note>The Marquis de Louvois (1641–1691), the French Secretary of State for War</note>. We know too that the eagle renews its plumage annually, and it is not easy to imagine how this could happen without a total change of its <term>juices</term> <note>original: "Juices"; in the medical theory of the time, this referred to the bodily humors or vital fluids</note>. The stag is another long-lived animal, though I cannot believe many of the stories told about it. I mention it only to observe that it sheds its antlers annually, which is another proof of the renewal of animal fluids. But this is even more obvious in the viper, which sheds its skin in the spring and emerges as youthful as the season itself. If the eagle, the stag, and the viper were not common to every climate, I have no doubt that the facts reported about them would be treated as fables; but because they happen every day and before our very eyes, we are forced to accept them as truths. 

Yet, what use have we made of these truths? I had almost forgotten an objection which has been raised from a supposed law of nature regarding animals—namely, that they have their appointed times, and that most of them have very short lifespans. The great <term>Lord Bacon</term> <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626), an English philosopher and scientist who wrote "The History of Life and Death," exploring how to prolong human life.</note> has taken much pains to establish this point, though with a better goal than simply supporting such an objection. <meta>This page continues the argument against the idea that human life is strictly limited by nature to a short term, using the progress of science as an analogy for the potential discovery of the secrets of longevity.</meta>

reach ten, and others, it is admitted, live much beyond a hundred; what does this have to do with the life of man? Why should it prove that we are limited to **eighty** <note>original: "Fourscore"</note> years, any more than it proves that we have it in our power to live as long as stags and eagles? It may be said that there is a certain progression in these periods, and that just as each animal has its assigned lifespan, so likewise does man. 

But we have proved the opposite of this by every method of argumentation yet invented. We showed it from **Scripture**, from reason, and from experience; and if all this is not sufficient to defeat a notion founded only on imagination, well then, let those who are fond of that idea enjoy it. I do not think that life ought to be forced upon people; my belief goes no further than this: *that if we seek, we may find; if we knock, it will be opened.* <note>A reference to the biblical verse Matthew 7:7.</note> I am led to say this because I find it to be true in other pursuits, where men follow nature and not their own preconceived notions. 

What mighty discoveries has **Newtonian philosophy** <note>The scientific principles and laws of motion established by Sir Isaac Newton (1642–1727).</note> provided, which would have been forever hidden from men if they had continued to follow the visions of **Descartes**? <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), a French philosopher whose scientific theories were largely replaced by Newton's findings.</note> How much more certain is the astronomy of our times than that of our ancestors? If, therefore, after everything that has been said, there still remain any doubts in the mind of the reader, we shall endeavor by all means to remove them. We do this both for the sake of the reader's satisfaction and so that, after so much effort already taken, we do not fail in our attempt and have this dissertation considered only as a literary amusement; on the contrary, we intend it as a serious and useful discourse. 

The ancients—who seem to have studied this matter with the greatest diligence <note>original: "Assiduity"</note> and whose opinions, generally speaking, do not appear altogether unreasonable to modern thinkers upon mature examination—observed that the causes of old age are chiefly three. 

First is the surrounding <note>original: "Circumambient"</note> air, which dries up the natural moisture in man at the same time that the internal <note>original: "innate"</note> heat of the body consumes it, as it is the necessary substance on which the flame of life must feed. 

The second is the toil and motion of the body, which likewise wastes that vital <note>original: "aerial"</note> humidity which is so necessary to health and life. 

The last is the passions of the mind, which according to the sentiments of the learned ***Avicenna*** <note>Avicenna (c. 980–1037) was a Persian physician and philosopher, considered one of the most significant thinkers of the Islamic Golden Age.</note>, have a greater influence than both the other causes taken together. This will seem extremely reasonable if we consider how close a connection there is between the passions of the mind and the motion of the animal spirits <note>In early medicine, "animal spirits" were thought to be a subtle fluid or essence that traveled through the nerves to facilitate sensation and movement.</note>. Those who are acquainted with that mysterious kind of philosophy <note>The author is likely referring to Hermeticism or Paracelsianism, which emphasized the connection between man and the universe.</note>, which was held in the highest credit by the educated public <note>original: "learned Vulgar"</note> of the last age, and which is now as unreasonably discredited among the same people—and regarded only by those who seek after truths rather than popular opinions—this philosophy, I say, teaches us that there is a great correspondence between the body of the Earth and the body of man. Hence it comes to pass that, just as man suffers and is destroyed for want of moisture, so this is likely to be the case for the world too—or at least would be the case if it did not receive external <note>original: "foreign"</note> supplies. We must allow this to be a rational and probable account of what for many ages puzzled the understanding of all mankind. If such a wonderful method as this is necessary to supply the Earth with moisture, why should we think it strange that a new and, in some measure, unaccustomed way should be the most fit for repairing that humidity which is so necessary to the well-being of the human body? 

The universe is under the special care and direction of its infinitely wise Creator, who at certain seasons has therefore provided it with such requisite supplies. But the bodies of men are, in this respect, left to their own care. If the materials are within our own reach, and our faculties are strong enough to discover them, we have no more right to complain that the secret of preserving long life is not discovered to us, than that the art of baking bread, of melting ores and refining metals, or indeed any other art, was not revealed to our ancestors in the earliest ages of the world, but was instead left to be the reward of their industry <note>In this context, "industry" refers to diligent hard work and application.</note> and sagacity <note>original: "Sagacity"; meaning wisdom, shrewdness, or keen mental discernment.</note>.

We now have the clue in our hand, and nothing can hinder us from extricating ourselves from this labyrinth of doubts about the proper method of obtaining this supply, except our own negligence and want of attention. We ought to remember that heat and moisture are, from the very beginning, the principles of human life. Let us reflect, then, and see if we cannot discover where these principles arise. There is, I think, very little difficulty in this case: heat is derived from the male, and moisture from the female. It is equally unnecessary and unbecoming for me to introduce a treatise on reproduction <note>original: "Treatise of Generation"</note> in a work like this; it is sufficient that I remind my readers that the development <note>original: "enlargement"</note> of the fetus in the womb requires a very extraordinary degree of moisture in all respects, and that this is entirely derived from its mother.

mother. The growth of an infant is surprisingly quick, and this is due to the strength of the vital flame <note>original: "Vital Flame"; in early medicine, this was the innate heat of the body believed to be the source of life and health.</note>, the constant supply of appropriate moisture, and not being exposed to those accidents mentioned before—namely, the action of the surrounding air, physical movement, and the passions of the mind. From all of these, children are largely free while in the womb. This, I think, is so extremely clear and obvious that it is simply impossible for a man to be confused about it. Therefore, I shall proceed to the next stage of life and examine the method nature pursues there.

In the state of infancy, a special supply of this moist matter is also necessary. It must be sufficient not only for performing the ordinary functions of biological life <note>original: "animal Life"; in this context, it refers to the basic physiological processes shared by humans and animals, such as breathing and circulation.</note> but also to facilitate the growth of the child. This supply, too, comes from the mother. Nature has provided her with breasts and with milk, which is the most suitable nutrition that the human body can receive under such circumstances. In extreme old age, when the body is reduced a second time to the weakness of infancy, milk—and especially women's milk—is found to be of very great use; in cases of consumption <note>original: "consumptive Cases"; consumption was a general term for wasting diseases, most commonly tuberculosis.</note>, it is also recognized as an excellent restorative. People in the final stage of life are frequently emaciated <note>extremely thin and weak.</note>, and in this case, milk may help. But there is still a wide difference between the natural and gradual decay of the human body and what physicians call consumption <note>original: "Consumption"; a general term for wasting diseases, most commonly tuberculosis.</note>; consequently, there must be a difference in their cure. Thus, from certain and almost self-evident principles, I have established the reasonableness of this proposition: that the breath and insensible perspiration <note>original: "insensible Perspiration"; the invisible loss of moisture through the skin and lungs, which was thought to carry vital spirits or "atoms" from one person to another.</note> of virgins in a young and healthy state must be very beneficial for old men and may very probably be a means of protecting them from those infirmities which usually accompany an advanced age. --- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his theory regarding the prolongation of life through the "breath of virgins," specifically addressing how his interpretation aligns with natural reason regardless of the historical accuracy of the original inscription.</meta>

It matters not at all whether the inscription I began with <note>referring to the legendary funerary inscription of Hermippus, who allegedly lived to 115 years by being constantly surrounded by the breath of young girls.</note> is founded in truth or not; nor is it necessary to believe that I have correctly identified the exact method used by *Hermippus*. The single point in question is whether I have shown that the sense in which I interpret this inscription is consistent with the dictates of reason and the laws of nature; and as to this, every reader must decide for himself. Before he does this, there is another kind of evidence that he ought to hear, and which I shall readily produce for the sake of truth.

There are two types of people whose interest it is particularly to criticize this doctrine, on the assumption that it is destructive to their own, to which they are excessively—and perhaps unreasonably—devoted: I mean the astrologers and hermetic philosophers <note>original: "Hermetic Philosophers"; followers of a mystical and alchemical tradition based on writings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, often seeking the "Philosopher's Stone" or "Elixir of Life."</note>. I shall examine what both may object, more for the entertainment and satisfaction than for the conviction of the wiser part of the world, who are already well enough aware of the credibility due to these *virtuosi* <note>original: "Virtuosi"; a term for learned men or amateur scientists, used here with a touch of irony regarding their eccentric theories.</note>. When I have done this, I shall move quickly to a conclusion, confident that nothing more can be necessary to establish the probability of this method for preserving health and life, and for defending our bodies from the infirmities of old age as long as their constitutions will permit.

The modern supporters of astrology—for it seems there are such people—will probably claim that this

<summary>The author argues that his theory on longevity is consistent with reason and nature, regardless of the inscription's historical accuracy, and prepares to address objections from astrologers and alchemists.</summary>
<keywords>Hermippus, longevity, astrology, Hermetic philosophy, Virtuosi, health, old age</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of potential objections to his theory, specifically from astrologers who might claim that a long life is the result of a favorable birth chart rather than his proposed method of inhaling the "breath of virgins."</meta>

The notion of *Hermippus* <note>Referring to the legendary figure who supposedly lived to 115 years.</note> was a mere whim <note>original: "Fancy"</note>, and that if he really reached such an advanced age, it was not through the help derived from the breath of virgins, but from the lucky position of the stars and planets at the time of his birth. It is, however, fortunate for me that they can never prove this objection, because I assume their art <note>referring to astrology</note> will not provide them with the means of constructing a horoscope <note>original: "Scheme of his Nativity"</note> for him. I know they may cite the authority of *Thomas Aquinas* <sup>q</sup> <note>a highly influential 13th-century philosopher and theologian</note>, and other great men who have supported these opinions. I also know that they may provide some extraordinary examples of confirmed astrological predictions; particularly those of *Basil*, who was so famous in *Florence*, and of *La Brosse* in *Paris*, who were especially successful at guessing future events and having their predictions recorded by reliable historians.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The first of these foretold to *Cosimo de' Medici*, then a private citizen in *Florence*, that he would achieve a very high rank, because the astrological sign rising at his birth <note>original: "Ascendant of his Nativity"</note> was blessed with the same favorable alignments as those of the Emperors *Augustus* and *Charles V* had been; and he was accordingly raised to the Dukedom of *Tuscany*,

---

<sup>q</sup> One strong passage among many in his writings runs thus: "Whoever knows the powers of the heavens and stars at the moment something is born could judge the nature of that thing, although this does not impose necessity and could be hindered by chance." <note>original Latin: *Qui Sciret virtutes Cælorum & Stellarum dum res aliqua nascitur posset judicare de natua rei, licet hoc neceſſitatem non imponat, & poſſet impediri per Accidens.* From St. Thomas Aquinas's commentary on the second book of *On Generation and Corruption*.</note>

in the month of *January* 1534. The same *Greek* astrologer <note>Basil, mentioned on the previous page.</note> foretold the death of Prince *Alessandro de' Medici* <note>original: "Alexander de Medici"</note> with equal skill. With this description, he practically pointed his finger at *Lorenzino de' Medici* <note>original: "Laurence de Medici"</note>, who murdered Prince *Alessandro* in his bedchamber, violating all laws of family <note>original: "Consanguinity"</note> and hospitality, in the year 1537§. 

However, the great misfortune is that back then, people strongly suspected <note>original: "shrewdly suspected"</note> that these so-called sages had better and more reliable ways of uncovering conspiracies than through astrology. I must admit that I am inclined to suspect, based on the very manner in which this prediction was delivered, that *Basil* was actually employed to warn Prince *Alessandro* about his cousin *Lorenzino*. Unfortunately, for lack of insight <note>original: "Penetration"</note>, the prince fell into the trap he might have otherwise avoided.

The case of *La Brosse* <note>original: "La Broffe"</note> is more relevant to our purpose because it is better documented; indeed, I think

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological predictions, moving from the Medici family in Italy to the case of the Baron de Biron in France.</meta>

that it is the best-documented story of its kind. The Baron de Biron (who later became the famous Marshal of that name), while facing some trouble regarding a duel, visited the astrologer La Brosse. He brought him his birth chart <term>original: "Scheme of his Nativity"; a horoscope based on the time of one’s birth</term> but claimed it belonged to a friend of his. After studying the chart, the astrologer assured him that the person to whom it belonged would undoubtedly become a great man. He even suggested the person might become a king, were it not for the *Caput Algol* <note>original: "Caput Algol"; the "Head of the Demon" or Medusa's star, which in traditional astrology was often associated with the threat of decapitation.</note> pointing toward the figure of the Dragon’s Head <term>The "Dragon’s Head," or the North Node of the Moon, was often used in astrology to signify destiny or points of intense focus.</term> in the chart. 

Monsieur de Biron, who did not understand the term, demanded a clearer explanation. "Well then," La Brosse replied, "because of a desire to be king, this man will do something that will cost him his head." Enraged by this answer, the Baron beat him mercilessly. However, he lived to see the prediction come true; as the whole world knows, he was eventually beheaded for a conspiracy against King Henry IV.†

No matter how much these types of ideas have been discredited by the rise of experimental science and genuine knowledge, whenever a prince is willing to listen to his own praise, there will always be people ready to use astrological charts—as well as other things—to flatter his vanity. Nothing is easier than to put such a spin on a situation like this; men of lively intelligence...

---

...know how to dress up the "Face of Heaven" upon such an occasion, and to properly arrange all the signs and planets so as to raise mighty expectations in the world, as well as the highest pleasure in the mind of the prince they flatter.

Such were the favorable influences of the celestial orbs at the birth of the late *Louis XIV*, King of *France*. The birth-chart system <term>original: "Genethliac System"; the branch of astrology dealing with the birth of individuals</term> may be seen in one of the medals that record the history of that reign. The gentlemen of the Royal Academy of Inscriptions <note>A French learned society founded in 1663 to preserve the history of the King through monuments and medals.</note> observed the precise position of the planets at the moment of that prince's birth. Around this curious medal, one sees the twelve signs of the Zodiac forming the twelve "houses" of this system. The seven planets appear in the same degrees they occupied at that time. The Sun, which gives perfection to the other planets, is in the "Midheaven" <term>the highest point in the sky at the time of birth, associated with career and status</term>. *Mars*, the "Lord of the Ascendant" <term>the planet ruling the sign that was rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth</term>, is in "reception" with *Jupiter*, the protector of life; this is what they call "the Great Fortune." *Saturn*, often called the "Enemy of Nature," is in his "dignities" <term>astrological positions where a planet is strong or "at home"</term>, which makes him less malevolent. The *Moon* is in conjunction with *Venus* and *Mercury*. *Mercury*, being in his favored "house" and ten degrees away from the Sun—thus remaining out of "combustion" <term>original: "Combustion"; an astrological state where a planet is so close to the Sun that its power is "burnt up" or obscured</term> and instead enlightened by the Sun's rays—grants a superiority of genius in the most difficult and most important enterprises. Even the fact that he is in a "square" aspect <term>original: "Quartile"; a 90-degree angle between planets, usually considered a sign of conflict or challenge</term> with *Mars* is not enough to diminish this quality. The birth chart <term>original: "Nativity"</term> of *Louis*...

<meta>This page continues the description of a commemorative medal for the birth of Louis XIV, specifically detailing its imagery and the astrological justification for his reign's success.</meta>

Louis XIV was depicted in the center of the medal <term>a commemorative coin-like object used to record historical events</term> as a rising Sun. The King is placed in the chariot of that glorious planet, which Ovid described in his poetry. This chariot is drawn by four horses and guided by Victory. The inscription contains these words: *The Rising of the Gallic Sun* <note>original Latin: "Ortus Solis Gallici"</note>. The bottom section <term>original: "Exergue"; the space on a coin or medal below the main design, often used for dates or inscriptions</term> contains these other Latin words: *September 5th, 38 minutes before noon, 1638* <note>original Latin: "Septembris quinto minutis 38 ante Meridiem, 1638"</note>.

I must admit that this is a clever invention and skillfully put together; yet I dare say that none of the learned gentlemen involved in creating this medal dared to predict any of the clouds that would later overshadow their "Sun." No, they were far too skilled as courtiers for that. 

But if this famous medal gives any credibility to astrology, I will venture to offer a few observations to protect my own theories from any criticism. I note then that just as the most fortunate birth charts <term>original: "Nativities"; astrological charts showing the positions of the planets at the moment of someone's birth</term> rely on natural causes to help bring about what they predict, it is not at all improbable that—if by some lucky chance we could see the horoscope of Hermippus <note>Hermippus Redivivus, a figure from a famous 18th-century text who allegedly lived to 115 by inhaling the breath of young women.</note>—it would show us how Mercury was well-positioned at the time of his birth. If Mercury was viewed by the Moon <note>original: "Luna"</note> with a favorable alignment <term>original: "Aspect"; the angular relationship between planets that astrologers believe influences their effect</term>, it might have led to his discovery of this great secret. This would have enabled him to use the "breath of virgins" <note>An archaic medical theory suggesting that the breath of the young could rejuvenate the elderly.</note> in a way that no man ever

<meta>This page concludes the author’s critique of astrologers and moves into a discussion of Hermetic philosophers—practitioners of alchemy—and their claims regarding a "Universal Medicine" for extending human life.</meta>

...they are gainers by the dispute; since, once truth is discovered, they have as much right to it as the person who found it. In the present case, if this treatise of mine should inspire any wiser man to look for a better solution to the problem I have stated, and he should luckily happen upon the genuine method of *Hermippus*, I could not rejoice at it more than he would. Nor would he find anyone more willing to acknowledge or to applaud his ingenuity. Let us read, let us meditate, let us debate, but always for the sake of truth. Truth is the great property of mankind; it constitutes all our happiness, and it is therefore in our common interest to pursue it. The goal I now have in view is to discover the means of prolonging life without feeling the infirmities of age. If the publication of this treatise contributes in any way to that discovery, then not only my purpose but the purpose of all mankind is served. A most noble point of science will be illustrated from what many might at first consider a pointless dispute.

I shall not treat the Hermetic philosophers <term>original: "Hermetic Philosophers"; followers of a philosophical and religious tradition based on writings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, often associated with alchemy and the pursuit of secret knowledge</term> quite as dismissively as I have the astrologers, because there have undoubtedly been many excellent people among them. An Adept wrote to Doctor *Edmund Dickinson*, Physician to King *Charles II* and an avowed admirer of Hermetic philosophy <term>original: "Hermetic Philosophy"; a tradition of esoteric wisdom involving alchemy, astrology, and magic</term>. The Doctor's letter is very plain and clear. His friend gives him a very plausible answer to all his objections. To strengthen the belief in what he proposes, he reminds the Doctor that he had performed "projection" <term>original: "Projection"; the final stage of the alchemical process where a small amount of the Philosopher's Stone is added to a base metal to transmute it into gold</term>—that is, he had changed base metals into gold—more than once in the Doctor's presence in the King's laboratory in *Whitehall*. ...so, let us look at some well-attested accounts <note>original: "Relations"</note> that seem to favor the truth of it.

In the year 1687, a strange incident occurred in *Venice* that caused quite a stir at the time, and which I believe deserves to be saved from being forgotten <note>original: "secured from Oblivion"</note>. The great freedom and ease with which all people of respectable appearance live in that city is well known to anyone familiar with it. Readers will not, therefore, be surprised that a stranger, who went by the name of *Signor Gualdi* and who made a considerable impression there, was admitted into the best circles of society, even though nobody knew who or what he was. He remained in *Venice* for several months, and three things were remarked regarding his conduct.

The first was that he possessed a small collection of fine paintings, which he readily showed to anyone who requested to see them. The second was that he was perfectly skilled in all the arts and sciences, and spoke on every subject with such readiness and insight that he astonished everyone who heard him. Third, it was observed that he never wrote or received any letters; he never requested credit or used bills of exchange <term>original: "Bills of Exchange"; a written order once used in international trade that bound one party to pay a fixed sum of money to another at a predetermined date, acting like a modern check or bank draft</term>, but instead paid for everything in ready money. He lived respectably, though not in luxury <note>original: "Splendor"</note>.

One day, this gentleman met a Venetian nobleman at a coffee-house who was an exceptionally good judge of paintings. This nobleman had heard of *Signor Gualdi’s* collection, and in a very polite...

...tions, he became acquainted with a chemist named ***Starkey***, before whom he performed a projection <note>term: projection; the final stage of the alchemical process where a small amount of the Philosopher’s Stone is added to a base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</note>. However, finding that ***Starkey*** was a corrupt and extravagant man, he broke off his acquaintance with him without sharing any of his secrets <sup>z</sup>.

It might be argued by those who view this entire affair as an idle and ridiculous thing—considering these accounts, however well-attested or supported, to be mere dreams or visions—that there is direct proof that their claims of long life are false. These angry critics may point out that we have distinct records of when their most celebrated patriarchs, such as ***Roger Bacon*** <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292) was a medieval English philosopher and friar often associated with early science and alchemy.</note>, ***Raymond Lully*** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher and mystic to whom many alchemical works were falsely attributed.</note>, and ***Basil Valentine*** <note>Basil Valentine was the legendary 15th-century author of several influential alchemical texts, though his historical existence is debated.</note>, died and were buried. Therefore, if these men could not preserve their own lives or even extend them beyond the ordinary span, what probability is there, these people ask, that any member of the brotherhood <note>original: "Fraternity"</note> could prolong their lives in the manner they describe? 

In response to this, the **adepts** <note>term: adept; a person who has mastered the secrets of alchemy and the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone.</note> always suggest that if these great men died, it was by their own choice, and that many others in their brotherhood still refuse that length <note>The catchword "length" from the bottom of the original page indicates the sentence likely concludes "length of years" or "length of life."</note>...

It was there that he became acquainted with ***Philalethes***, an acquaintance he boasts about frequently in his writings. In 1658, he published a book on chemistry in ***London***, which was translated into ***French*** in 1706 and into ***German*** <note>original: "High Dutch"</note> in 1712.

...the length of life their art would enable them to enjoy. I must confess that this is an unsatisfactory answer, especially to those who raise the objection. However, the *Adepts* <note>term: Adept; a person who has mastered the secrets of alchemy and the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone.</note> say it is the only answer appropriate for them to give. They claim, "We do not aim to convince these sorts of people; we do not desire to cause a stir in the world. We do not even regard the possession of riches and long life as blessings in themselves, except insofar as they enable us to do good. The only reason we ever mention that we possess such secrets is to attract worthy people to our society who deserve to be included." 

Now, however weak or sophisticated <note>original: "sophistical"; here meaning reasoning that sounds clever but is actually flawed or deceptive.</note> this reasoning may appear to the general public, it must be allowed that, based on the principles of *Hermetic philosophy* <note>term: Hermetic philosophy; a mystical and alchemical tradition based on the writings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, it is extremely plausible. In all such cases, we must allow men to argue from their own principles rather than from the ones we impose.

As I do not claim to be an *Adept*, or even a *student* of this kind of philosophy, I will take the liberty—since the facts closely concern the subject I am treating—to formulate another answer to this objection. It is this: perhaps we are not always as certain about the deaths of these *virtuosi* <note>term: virtuosi; individuals with a deep interest or skill in the arts and sciences; here specifically referring to alchemists.</note> as we imagine. The continuation of their lives is something that, although they boast of it in one sense, yet...

<meta>signature: Q</meta>

...yet in another way, they studiously attempt to conceal it. I shall give an extraordinary *instance* <note>original: "Instance"; here meaning an example or case study.</note> of this, which will be all the more entertaining to the reader because, as far as I know, it has not yet been noticed by any writer on this subject—not even by the self-proclaimed historian of this brotherhood, even though he has gathered more information about them and their philosophy than any man who has ever set pen to paper. 

This *example* will also be found more extraordinary because I take it from someone who never claimed to be an *Adept* <note>term: Adept; a person who has mastered the secrets of alchemy, specifically the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone.</note>, and who should therefore be considered an unbiased *witness*. But before I begin this story, I must ask my readers to note that I am simply presenting the facts as I find them, without intending to involve myself in the least regarding the credibility they may receive.

Among the *Hermetic Philosophers* <note>term: Hermetic philosophy; a mystical and alchemical tradition based on the writings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note> who are acknowledged to have reached the highest secrets of science, *Nicholas Flamel* of Paris has always been considered one of the most significant, and his right to this reputation is the least disputed. The history of this *Flamel*, who lived in the *14th* century, is very curious: he was from a good family, though his fortune had been greatly reduced. He possessed quick intelligence and a lively wit; with the benefit of only a basic education, he was sent to Paris to make a living.

<summary>The author introduces Nicholas Flamel, a famous 14th-century Parisian alchemist, as a prime example of an "Adept" whose life and secrets were documented by an unbiased source.</summary>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Nicholas Flamel, describing his early struggles in Paris and his discovery of the mysterious alchemical book by "Abraham the Jew."</meta>

...as he could. *Flamel* had extraordinarily good handwriting, some knowledge of poetry, and painted quite well; yet all these accomplishments raised him no higher than a common clerk <term>original: "hackney clerk"; a clerk who performs routine, menial writing tasks for hire</term>. In this position, he worked very hard and struggled greatly to earn a basic living. In 1357, chance threw in his way a book of *Hermetic Philosophy* <note>term: Hermetic philosophy; a mystical and alchemical tradition based on the writings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, written by someone called *Abraham the Jew*. It was actually engraved on leaves made from the bark of trees and illustrated with very strange pictures, in which the whole secret was laid down in the clearest manner possible—at least to those who were already acquainted with Hermetic philosophy. This treasure cost *Flamel* no more than two florins <note>term: Florin; a gold or silver coin used in Europe during the Middle Ages.</note>, for the person who sold him the book knew nothing of its contents. Even though *Flamel* made it his entire study for twenty years, and although he took the precaution of copying the pictures, hanging them up in his house, and asking scholars for their opinions on them, he was able to understand very little of them<sup>a</sup>.

TIRED at length with such a futile and laborious study, he took a resolution in 1378...

---

<sup>a</sup> The best part of this account of Nicholas Flamel is taken from his entry in a very unusual book, which deserves to be better known than it is. This encourages me to provide the full title, which is as follows: *Treasury of Researches and Antiquities: Gallic [French] history reduced into alphabetical order and enriched with many origins, epitaphs, and other rare and curious things, as well as many words of the Thyoise or Teutonic language.* <note>original: "Trésor des Recherches & Antiquitez, Gauloises réduites en ordre alphabetique & enriches de beaucoup d'origines épitaphes & autres choses rares & curieuses, comme aussi de beaucoup de mots de la langue Thyoise, ou Theuthfranque."</note> Paris, 1655, in quarto <note>term: Quarto; a book size where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves (eight pages).</note>.

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Nicholas Flamel, recounting his journey to Spain to find a scholar capable of deciphering the mysterious book of "Abraham the Jew."</meta>

...to travel into *Spain*, in hopes of meeting a learned *Jewish* scholar <note>original: "Jew"</note> there who might give him the key to the "Grand Secret" <note>The secret of the Philosopher's Stone and the transmutation of metals.</note>. So that this journey would not appear to be motivated by something so unrealistic <note>original: "chimerical"</note>, he made a vow to go on a pilgrimage to St. *James* of *Compostella* <note>Santiago de Compostela, a major shrine in Spain and a popular pilgrimage destination.</note>, a common practice in those times. After searching extensively with little success, he finally met a *Jewish* physician in *Leon* who had recently converted to the Christian religion and was well-versed in this kind of science. At *Flamel’s* persuasion, this man agreed to travel with him to *Paris*; but when they reached *Orleans*, the physician—who was advanced in years and unaccustomed to the hardships of travel—fell ill with a fever, which killed him in a few days<sup>b</sup>.

*Flamel*, having performed the final acts of kindness for his dying friend, returned to *Paris* in great distress. There, he studied for three more years, following the instructions he had received from the physician. He met with such success that on January 17, 1382, he performed a "projection" <term>Projection: the final stage of the alchemical process, where the "philosopher's stone" is cast onto base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</term> on a large quan— <meta>catchword: tity</meta>

---

<sup>b</sup> As the history of Flamel was compiled long after his death, it is quite possible that some mistakes were made regarding the specific details of his adventures. However, there is one piece of evidence that I believe proves the truth of the story beyond dispute: this very "Book of Abraham the Jew," along with *Flamel’s* annotations (written based on the instructions he received from the physician), was actually owned by Cardinal Richelieu. This was reported by Pierre Borel, who was told by the Count de Cabrines that he had seen and examined the book himself.

<meta>This page continues the biography of Nicholas Flamel, describing his successful alchemical transmutations and the charitable uses of his resulting wealth.</meta>

-tity of mercury, which he changed into fine silver, and on the 25th of April following, he transmuted <term>Transmuted: To change from one nature, substance, or form into another; in alchemy, turning base metals into gold.</term> a vast quantity of mercury into gold. He afterwards repeated the experiment frequently and acquired immense wealth by doing so. He and his wife, Perrenella, in the midst of all these riches, continued to live in their old, sober way, eating and drinking as usual out of earthen vessels <note>original: "Earthen Vessels"; simple clay dishes rather than the gold or silver plates their wealth could have afforded.</note>. They supported, however, a vast number of poor people, founded fourteen hospitals, built three chapels, and repaired and endowed seven churches. In short, the acts of charity they performed were so astonishing that King Charles VI <note>Charles VI "the Beloved" (reigned 1380–1422), King of France during the Hundred Years' War.</note>, who was then on the throne, resolved to inquire how they obtained their wealth. For that purpose, he sent Monsieur de Cramoisi, a Master of Requests <term>Master of Requests: A high-ranking judicial officer in the French royal household who handled petitions to the King.</term> and a magistrate of the highest reputation for integrity and honor, to examine their circumstances. Flamel gave him such a satisfactory answer that no further inquiry was made about them; instead, the honest old couple was left in possession of the only privilege they desired, which was no greater than that of doing all the good that lay in their power<sup>c</sup>.

<sup>c</sup> This, too, is an undisputed fact; and as proof that Flamel drew his riches from his knowledge of Hermetic philosophy <term>Hermetic Philosophy: The study of alchemy and occult sciences based on writings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</term>, the hieroglyphic pictures upon his tomb are usually, and I think very justly, cited. <meta>This page continues the biography of the legendary alchemist Nicholas Flamel, moving from the description of his charitable works to the historical evidence for his wealth and the fate of his alchemical secrets.</meta>
