The circumstances of this story—the immense wealth of **Flamel** and his wife, their many charitable foundations, their vast endowments, and the enormous estate they left behind—are all facts so well-documented that no dispute can be raised about them. Even if there were a dispute, the last will of **Nicholas Flamel**, along with forty authentic legal records of his many charitable foundations (which are kept in the archives of the Parish Church of **Saint James of the Butchery** <note>original: "St. James, in the Butchery"; referring to the church of Saint-Jacques-de-la-Boucherie in Paris, of which only the tower remains today.</note> in **Paris**), provides proof capable of convincing the greatest skeptic <note>original: "Infidel"; used here to mean someone who stubbornly doubts the historical record.</note>. 

This **Flamel** wrote several treatises on the art of chemistry <note>In the 17th and 18th centuries, "chemistry" and "alchemy" were often used interchangeably.</note>, but they are extremely obscure because they are written entirely in an allegorical way. Consequently, one might find various interpretations without ever discovering the true one. It is said that he gave the true secret to a nephew of his and that it remained in the family for a long time; indeed, it is only due to a lack of discretion if it is not still held by them <gloss>d</gloss>. However, I must not hide the fact that there has been an attempt to overturn this entire history. This was not done by denying the facts, for that would have been ridiculous, since there are hundreds of poor people who...

---

<gloss>d</gloss> **Flamel** left his secret to the **Du Perrier** family. The last member of that family was a physician of the same name, among whose papers a man named **Du Bois** found it—or rather, he found only part of the "powder" <note>The "Powder of Projection," a substance used by alchemists to transmute base metals into gold.</note>. Having acted very imprudently by performing a "projection" <term>Projection: The final stage of the alchemical process where the philosopher's stone or powder is added to base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</term> in front of several people and pretending to have much greater knowledge than he actually possessed, **Du Bois** brought himself to an unfortunate end: he was hanged by order of **Cardinal Richelieu** <note>Armand Jean du Plessis, Cardinal-Duke of Richelieu (1585–1642), the powerful chief minister to France's King Louis XIII.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of Nicholas Flamel's wealth, specifically refuting the claim that he stole his fortune from Jewish people during their expulsion from France, rather than acquiring it through alchemy.</meta>

still live on the foundations established by **Flamel** and his wife, and they are therefore so many living witnesses to the truth of that part of the account.

BUT there has been an attempt to give another account of **Flamel**’s acquisition of his wealth. To this end, they tell you that he was a public notary at the time the **Jews** were expelled from **France** <note>A reference to the mass expulsion of Jewish people from France by King Charles VI in 1394.</note>, and that they deposited a large part of their wealth with him in trust, which he then kept for his own use <sup>e</sup>. Those who treat everything said about the **Philosopher's Stone** and **Hermetic Philosophy** as a fable have accepted this explanation as if it were a clear and satisfactory account of the matter, without considering that it is, in truth, accompanied by greater difficulties than the story of the chemical transmutation. For what probability is there that people of such worth and piety as **Flamel** and his wife are acknowledged to have been would be guilty of such a flagrant act of injustice as to betray the trust placed in them—and to do so purely to perform acts of charity? If, indeed, they had lived in luxurious plenty and had reveled in all the pleasures which their immense wealth

However, to imagine that two sober people, leading a life of the utmost frugality and spending all their income on pious and charitable purposes, would devise a way to obtain the money spent through base and fraudulent means is utterly incredible. 

Besides, if this were the case, it is impossible to account for two circumstances. The first is that the King of **France** was satisfied with the explanation that **Flamel** chose to give to **Cramoisi** <note>Monsieur de Cramoisi was a royal official sent by King Charles VI to investigate the source of Flamel's wealth.</note>. But because I know there are people who—to avoid being suspected of gullibility—will believe anything that opposes what they think would be shameful to believe, I shall destroy this critical history of **Flamel**’s riches with an argument that even these people dare not discredit. In short, the **Jews** were expelled from **France** twice: first in 1180, long before **Flamel** was born, and again in 1406, several years after all his endowments <note>original: "Endowments"; permanent gifts of money or property to an institution like a hospital or church.</note> were already made, and only seven years before the death of him and his wife, **Perrenella** <note>original: "Perrenella"; usually spelled Perenelle, the wife and alchemical partner of Nicholas Flamel.</note> <sup>f</sup>.

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Nicholas Flamel’s reputation and begins to introduce a new, extraordinary account of his life—and potential immortality—sourced from the travelogues of Paul Lucas.</meta>

But I think I hear some skeptical reader exclaim: "Did **Flamel** and **Perenelle** die? What, then, is the point of all this long story? What is there in **Flamel**’s life that corresponds with that of **Hermippus**? Or what does alchemy have to do with the 'breath of virgins' or the prolongation of human life?"

Be patient for a moment; I promised you an account of **Flamel** that has not been considered until now—one that has escaped the notice of everyone who has written the history of Hermetic philosophers <note>original: "Hermetic Philosophers"; practitioners of alchemy and secret sciences based on the teachings of Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, from the noble **Olaus Borrichius** <note>Oluf Borch (1626–1690), a Danish polymath and chemist known for his defense of alchemy.</note> down to **Abbé du Fresnoy** <note>original: "Abbè du Frensoy"; referring to Nicolas Lenglet Du Fresnoy (1674–1755), a French scholar and author of a comprehensive history of alchemical philosophy.</note>. This is what I am about to share with you.

But first, permit me to note that my account is taken from the travels of **Monsieur Paul Lucas** <note>original: "Sieur Paul Lucas"; a French explorer and antiquarian (1664–1737) who traveled the Mediterranean and East on behalf of the French crown.</note>. By order of **Louis XIV**, he traveled through **Greece**, **Asia Minor**, **Macedonia**, and **Africa** in search of antiquities. He dedicated his book to that prince, and it must therefore be presumed that he related what was true, or what he believed to be true. No one who knew the character of **Louis XIV** could imagine that he would allow a man to introduce falsehoods to the world under the authority of his name. Much less would he encourage, protect, and employ such a man until the very end of his reign—as he actually did—as a result of the reputation Lucas acquired from the publication of

<meta>This page continues the introduction of Paul Lucas as a source for the ongoing story of Nicholas Flamel, establishing Lucas's character as a reliable, if unscientific, witness to extraordinary events.</meta>

[ 129 ]

...the *Voyages*, the authority of which I am going to use <note>The author is citing the travel logs of Paul Lucas as primary evidence.</note>.

<margin>g See the preface to the book from which this story is taken, which is entitled *Travels of Mr. Paul Lucas, made by Order of the King in Greece, etc.* <note>original: "Voyage de Sieur Paul Lucas fait par Ordre Du Roi dans La Grece, &c."</note> Amsterdam, 1714, in duodecimo <note>A small book size where each sheet is folded into twelve leaves.</note>, two volumes.</margin>

It is appropriate to remind my reader that I have strictly kept my word. **Monsieur Lucas** was no Hermetic philosopher <note>original: "Hermetic Philoſopher"; a practitioner of alchemy and secret sciences based on the teachings of Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, no chemist, and no deep student of the sciences. If we judge from his writings, he was not a man of great skill or social refinement, but rather a bold, rough, and plain-spoken traveler who had seen much and was willing to tell the world everything he had seen. 

If anyone is led to believe that he was a gullible man who might be easily deceived, I have nothing to say to that; I do not intend to defend the soundness of a traveler's intellect any more than I intend to defend the truth of Hermetic philosophy. I only present things as they are—or at least as they appear to be—and leave everything else to the reader's decision. All I insist upon regarding **Monsieur Lucas**'s account is this: he could not have been mistaken about the facts themselves. He could not have dreamed the story he has told us, nor could he have seen it in a vision. As for the rest, I do not concern myself with it; it is possible he was cheated by the Muslim monks <note>original: "Mohammedan Monks"; likely referring to dervishes or members of Sufi orders encountered during his travels in the Ottoman Empire.</note>, for I can easily imagine that monks...

<summary>The author argues for the reliability of Paul Lucas's travel accounts, asserting that while Lucas was not a scientist or an intellectual, his straightforward nature makes him a credible witness to the facts he observed.</summary>
<keywords>Paul Lucas, Hermetic philosophy, alchemy, travel writing, reliability, Muslim monks, 18th-century literature</keywords>

...of all religions are the same; and yet, if just as many absurdities and even greater difficulties accompany the story when viewed in this light than in any other, I believe it justifies suggesting that it is not impossible that things happened differently than such harsh critics are inclined to believe. But it is now time to get to the story, and therefore I shall end my reflections.

**He** informs us that while he was at *Bursa* <note>original: "Broussa"; a major city in northwestern Turkey.</note>, in *Anatolia* <note>original: "Natolia"; the peninsula making up most of modern Turkey.</note>, and was going out to get some fresh air <note>original: "take the Air"</note> toward a little village called *Pinarbasi* <note>original: "Bournous Backy"; likely the Pınarbaşı district near Bursa, known for its springs.</note> a short distance away, in the company of a person of high rank, he encountered the following adventure, which I shall relate to you exactly in his own words. 

"We went together to a little mosque," he says, "where one of their most eminent dervishes <note>original: "Dervises"; members of a Muslim religious order known for their vows of poverty and austerity.</note> was buried. It is always a dervish who is the custodian of such places, which are, generally speaking, pleasantly situated, adorned with gardens and fountains, and for that reason set apart as public walks and places for recreation. We were quickly introduced into a little cloister, where we found four dervishes who received us with the utmost politeness and asked us to share what they were eating. We were told—and soon found it to be very true—that they were all people of the greatest worth and learning. One of them, who said he was from the country of the *Uzbeks* <note>original: "Usbecks"</note> (a tribe of *Tatars* <note>original: "Tartars"</note>), appeared to me more learned..."

...than the rest, and I truly believe he spoke every language in the world. Since he did not know I was a **Frenchman**, after we had talked for some time in the **Turkish** language, he asked me if I could speak **Latin**, **Spanish**, or **Italian**. I told him that if he liked, he could speak to me in **Italian**; but as he soon discovered by my accent that it was not my native language, he asked me frankly what country I came from. As soon as he learned that I was a native of **France**, he spoke to me in as good **French** as if he had been raised in **Paris**. "How long, Sir," I asked, "did you stay in **France**?" He answered me that he had never been there, but that he had a strong desire <note>original: "Inclination"</note> to make the journey.

I did everything in my power to encourage that resolution and persuade him to go. To that end, I told him that there was no kingdom in the world more refined <note>original: "polished"</note>; that foreigners especially were extremely well received there, and that without a doubt, he would find the greatest satisfaction in this journey. People had given him bad impressions of my country; I told him that *France*, on the contrary, was the very nursery <note>original: "Nursery"; meaning a place of growth and education</note> of the learned, and that the King, whose subject I had the honor to be, was the greatest patron of the sciences. I went even further; I told him that although I did not have the honor to be of any learned profession myself, His Majesty was pleased to pay the expenses <note>original: "defray the Expences"</note> of the travels in which he saw me engaged. I explained that this was done with no other goal than to obtain information <note>original: "procure Notices"</note> about things whose knowledge was still necessary for perfecting the sciences. 

I mentioned things such as herbs that might be useful in medicine <note>original: "Physic"; the 18th-century term for the art of healing or medical science</note>, ancient monuments that might contribute to the illustration of remarkable events and consequently serve to make history more complete, and the inspection of the countries themselves in order to correct <note>original: "rectifying"</note> geographical charts. In short, I went through all the arguments <note>original: "Proofs"</note> I could think of to convince him of the passion for the sciences and learning that prevailed in *France*. He attributed all of this to the climate and seemed to agree with what I said out of pure politeness. 

At last, however, he seemed to be delighted <note>original: "ravished"</note> by the fine things I told him and went so far as to assure me that some time or other, he would certainly go there. Our conversation having ended, the *Dervishes* <note>original: "Dervises"; members of a Muslim religious order known for their asceticism and, in some traditions, mystical knowledge</note> took us to their house, which was at the bottom of the mountain, very near *Bournous* <note>original: "Bournous"; likely referring to the thermal baths of Bursa, such as the Eski Kaplıca.</note>.

On the 10th, the dervish <note>original: "Dervise"; a member of a Muslim religious order often associated with asceticism or mysticism</note> whom I had mistaken for an Uzbek <note>original: "Usbeck"; an ethnic group from Central Asia</note> came to visit me. I must say, in favor of this dervish, that he was an extraordinary person in every way, even down to his outward appearance. He showed me many curious things related to medicine <note>original: "Physic"; the 18th-century term for the art of healing or medical science</note> and promised to show me more. At the same time, however, he could not help saying that it was necessary for me to make some extraordinary preparations on my part to put myself in a condition to benefit from the insights <note>original: "Lights"; meaning intellectual or spiritual enlightenment</note> he was able to give me. 

Judging by his appearance, he seemed to be a man of about thirty years old; but based on his conversation, he seemed to have lived for at least a century. I was further convinced of this by the accounts he gave me of some long voyages he had undertaken.

He told me that he was one of seven friends who all wandered up and down the world with the same goal of perfecting themselves in their studies. He added that, upon parting, they always appointed another meeting at the end of twenty years.

In a long conversation with a man of great intellect, it is natural to cover a great many curious topics. At this point, I stopped him. "Despite all these fine maxims," I said, "the sage dies just like other people. What does it matter to me, then, whether I have been a fool or a philosopher, if wisdom has no advantage over folly, and one is no more of a shield against death than the other?"

"Alas!" he said, "I see you are absolutely unacquainted with the sublime sciences and have never known true philosophy. Learn from me, my friend: a person such as I have described does indeed die, for death is a debt which nature demands, and from which no man can be exempt. However, he does not die before the time fixed by his great Creator. 

But you must also observe that this lifespan approaches nearly a thousand years, and a sage may live for that entire length of time. He achieves this through the knowledge he has of the true medicine. By this means, he is able to ward off whatever may impair <note>original: "impeach"; here meaning to hinder or challenge the effectiveness of bodily health</note> or hinder his bodily functions <note>original: "Animal Functions"; referring to the basic biological processes required for life</note>, or destroy the balance of his physical constitution <note>original: "Temperature of his Nature"; referring to the historical medical theory of the four humors, where health depended on a balanced 'temperament' or 'temperature'</note>. ...erased them from his mind again; for having attained this kind of natural knowledge, he began to mix his own notions and ideas with it. Through this confusion, which was the result of a foolish curiosity, he made even the work of his Creator imperfect. It is this error that the sage <note>original: "Sage"; referring to a wise person or philosopher, specifically in the context of alchemy and hermetic wisdom</note> labors to correct. 

Other animals act only by their instinct, by which they preserve themselves just as they did at their first creation <note>original: "Institution"; in this context, the established order of nature at the beginning of the world</note>, and they live as long now as they did when the world first began. Humanity is much more perfect; but have we still preserved the prerogative mentioned before? Or have we not long ago lost the glorious privilege of living a thousand years, which we should have studied with such care to preserve? 

This, then, is what the true sages have recovered. So that you may no longer be mistaken, let me assure you that this is what they call the Philosopher’s Stone <note>original: "Philosophers Stone"; the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and, as described here, providing the "elixir of life" for extreme longevity</note>. It is not a mythical <note>original: "Chimerical"; imaginary, fantastic, or impossible</note> science, as some poorly-read people imagine, but something solid and sound. On the other hand, it is certainly known only to a few. Indeed, it is impossible for it to be revealed to most of humanity, whom greed or excess <note>original: "Debauch"; excessive indulgence in sensual pleasures that wears down the body</note> destroy, or whom an impulsive desire for life kills.

Surprised by all I heard, I said, "And would you then persuade me that all who have possessed the Philosopher’s Stone have also lived a thousand years?"

Our friend did not hesitate to reveal himself to his relative; on the contrary, he entered into a close friendship with him and provided him with a wealth of insights <note>original: "Lights," meaning intellectual or spiritual enlightenment in alchemical processes</note>.

However, because the "first matter" <note>The *materia prima*, the base starting material required for alchemical transformation, which was said to require lengthy and patient preparation</note> takes a long time to prepare, he contented himself with writing down the entire sequence of the process. To convince his nephew that he had not entertained him with falsehoods, he performed a projection <note>The final stage of the alchemical process where a small amount of the "Medicine" or Stone is cast upon base metal to transmute it</note> in his presence on thirty *okes* <note>original: "Ocques," a unit of weight used in the Ottoman Empire and surrounding regions; the text defines it here as three pounds</note> of base metal and turned it into pure gold.

The *Rabbi*, full of admiration, did everything he could to persuade our brother to stay with him, but it was in vain; for the traveler, on the other hand, was resolved not to break his promise to us. When the *Jew* realized this, his affection turned into deadly hatred. His greed, stifling all principles of human nature and religion, led him to resolve to extinguish one of the "lights of the universe" <note>A metaphorical reference to the alchemist as a rare source of divine knowledge</note>.

Hiding his dark intentions, however, he begged the sage in the most affectionate manner to stay with him for just a few more days. During this time, he plotted and carried out his wretched plan: he murdered our brother and took possession of his medicine <note>In alchemy, the "Medicine" or Elixir is the finished Philosopher's Stone in liquid or powder form</note>. Such horrible actions never remain unpunished for long. Other dark deeds he had committed came to light, for which the *Jew* was thrown into prison, convicted, and burned alive.

The *Jews* in *Paris* soon after fell under a persecution, as you have undoubtedly heard. *Flamel* <note>Nicolas Flamel (c. 1330–1418): A French scribe and manuscript seller who posthumously developed a reputation as a legendary alchemist who discovered the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, being more reasonable than the rest of his countrymen, developed a close friendship with some of them. Because his great honesty and unblemished integrity <note>original: "Probity"</note> were well known, a *Jewish* merchant entrusted him with all his books and papers. Among these were the papers belonging to the *Jew* who had been burned, as well as the book that our brother had left with him.

The merchant, no doubt occupied with his own affairs and the management of his trade, had never examined this valuable work with any attention. However, *Flamel*, whose curiosity led him to study it more closely, noticed several pictures of furnaces, alembics <note>Alembic: An early chemical vessel used for distillation, consisting of a gourd-like container and a cap with a long beak.</note>, and other vessels. He immediately began to understand <note>original: "apprehend"</note> that this book contained the "Grand Secret" <note>The Grand Secret: The formula for the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note>.

He had the first leaf of the book, which was in *Hebrew*, translated. Based on the little he found there, his suspicions were confirmed. But knowing that the matter required prudence and caution, he took the following steps to avoid any discovery. He traveled to *Spain*, and since *Jews* were settled everywhere throughout that country, he sought out the most learned scholars in every place he visited. He engaged each of them to translate a single page of his book. Having thus obtained a complete translation <note>original: "Version"</note>, he set out again for *Paris*. Fame is often a very dangerous evil, but a true Sage <note>Sage: In this context, a "Sophic" or wise master of alchemy who has achieved spiritual and material perfection.</note> knows how to free himself from all kinds of peril. *Flamel* saw clearly that the prevailing belief that he possessed the Philosopher's Stone might be fatal to both his liberty and his life. ---

<sup>h</sup> <margin>Note on the source of this story.</margin> The reader will easily notice that there are some variations in this history compared to the one we gave earlier regarding *Flamel*. However, this only shows that we have not done anything to falsely support this tale or to give it an air of probability that it honestly lacks. After all, the difference between these accounts is not so great as to destroy the credibility of either of them. It is very likely that Lucas’s <note>Paul Lucas (1664–1737): A French merchant and traveler who claimed to have met a group of dervishes in Turkey who told him that Flamel and his wife were still alive in the 18th century.</note> memory might have failed him, or that he might have refreshed it upon his return to *France* from the common story of *Flamel*, with which his account agrees. The principal reason I cited it was for his conclusion, which I particularly recommend to the reader's consideration; for if *Flamel’s* story is known in those parts of the world with such a degree of accuracy, it is no less wonderful than the rest of the story in all its circumstances.

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the legend of Nicolas Flamel, detailing the clever ruse he used to disappear from society and live in secret with his wife, Perenelle, after allegedly discovering the secret to immortality.</meta>

<page-num>[ 142 ]</page-num>

...a report of his wife's death and his own. Following his advice, she feigned illness from a distemper <note>original: "Distemper" — an old-fashioned term for a physical illness or disease.</note>, which followed its usual course. By the time she was said to have died, she had already reached the frontiers of Switzerland, where he had directed her to wait for him. They buried a wooden image dressed in her clothes in her place. To ensure nothing was missing from the ceremony, it was interred in one of the churches that they had founded. 

Some time later, he had recourse to a similar stratagem for his own security. Having buried another wooden statue, he joined his wife by the time the funeral was over. You will easily perceive that there was no great difficulty in all of this, since in every country, if a man has money, physicians and priests are always at his service, ready to say or do whatever he directs them. 

To give the matter an even better appearance and to prevent the least suspicion of the cheat, *Flamel* made his last will and testament in due form. In it, he particularly requested that his corpse might be interred near that of his dear wife, and that a pyramid <note>Historically, Flamel did design a complex tombstone (often called a "pyramid" in older texts) for himself and his wife at the Church of Saint-Jacques-de-la-Boucherie in Paris.</note> should be erected to their memories. Since that time, both of them have led a philosophic life <note>philosophic life: In the context of alchemy, this refers to a life of quiet study and contemplation, often implying the use of the Elixir of Life to achieve extreme longevity.</note>, sometimes in one country and sometimes in another. This, depend upon it, is the true history of *Flamel* and his wife, and not the version you have heard in *Paris*, where there are very few people who have ever had the slightest glimpse of true wisdom.

This story appeared to me, as I think it must appear to everyone, equally singular and strange, and all the more so because it was told to me by a *Muslim* <note>original: "Mohammedan" — an archaic term for a follower of Islam, now considered outdated.</note>, who I have every reason in the world to believe never set one foot in *France*. I will therefore content myself with saying that we are prone to hold too low an opinion of the learning of *Muslims*, for this man was certainly a person of extensive knowledge in all respects, and possessed a superior genius <note>original: "Genius" — in this context, it refers to an exceptionally high level of natural intelligence or mental capacity.</note>.<sup>i</sup>

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of Hermippus's method for extending life. He clarifies that while the method helps delay the decline of old age, it is not intended to extend the "youthful" period of a robust adult, as an excess of vital energy in a healthy body can be dangerous.</meta>

->[ 148 ]<-

...discomforts <note>original: "modities," likely the end of the word "discommodities," meaning disadvantages or troubles.</note> which usually accompany aging; yet I am far from believing that this method would contribute at all to the extension of youth, but rather the opposite. For this, I think I am able to offer several very probable reasons.

In the first place, it is necessary to observe that I do not mean "youth" to be a state of infancy or childhood, but rather that robust stage of life between twenty-five and forty. I believe that the social interaction <note>original: "conversation"; in this period, the term referred to living among or associating with others, not just speaking.</note> of very young people with each other is as healthy for their bodies as it is pleasant and agreeable to their minds. But when the human body has arrived at its full strength and is in that state of health which temperance <note>moderation, particularly in food and drink</note> and equanimity <note>mental calmness and evenness of temper</note> will maintain, a surplus <note>original: "surcharge"</note> of animal spirits <note>In early modern medicine, "spirits" were vital fluids or vapors thought to circulate through the body and sustain life and movement.</note> may not only prove useless, but dangerous. 

It is an old and very true observation that the most blooming <note>original: "florid"</note> state of health is the one in which a man is in the greatest peril if he receives any infection. The reason is obvious: namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, because the animal spirits then act with the utmost vigor and, consequently, must cause more mischief if they are tainted by any means. From this same method of reasoning, we may conclude that the lifestyle we have supposed *Hermippus* led might be accompanied by inconveniences to a man of a robust constitution, and perhaps incline him to fits of mania <note>original: "frenzies"</note>, or at least to fevers. A light diet, moderate exercise, and a perfect control over one's passions <note>strong emotions or physical desires</note>, along with a few minor remedies taken on proper occasions and as nature directs, may maintain a man in the full possession of health and vitality until age sixty. Then, it is time enough for him to think of avoiding the inconveniences which usually accompany old age. I might also observe that the social interaction <note>original: "Conversation"; here meaning living among or associating with others</note> of many young women might, in the prime <note>original: "Summer"</note> of life, bring along with it other complications <note>original: "Inconveniencies"</note>; but these are subjects on which I do not choose to dwell, because it is not easy to treat them with that delicacy which a philosophical discourse like this requires. Furthermore, the slightest hint is sufficient to suggest more to a man of good sense than is at all necessary to be stated on this subject.

I take it for granted, therefore, that I have assigned the proper limits to my remedy, and that I may safely define it as the restorative medicine <note>original: "Cordial," a medicinal drink intended to stimulate the heart and spirits</note> of advanced years. It can never be safely administered until a proper application of reason has produced a complete withdrawal of physical desire <note>original: "absolute retreat of Appetite"</note> ª.

But if anyone should be foolish enough to pursue this objection further and cry out, "What significance then is your remedy? Why, in such a situation, would you extend life at all, or what use are years when one is deprived of enjoyments?" If I say

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his method for longevity. He argues that the elderly should focus on intellectual pleasures and mentoring the young rather than physical desires or overly taxing academic studies.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>

If anyone should say there are some so wild as to talk in this manner, my answer is that they misunderstand my meaning. I am clearly of the opinion that the pleasures of the mind are far superior to all physical pleasures <note>original: "sensual Delights"</note>. Furthermore, I believe that cultivating youthful minds—which is the work I have imagined my elderly man engaged in—is a pleasant and noble undertaking. It is in every way worthy of the soul’s highest <note>original: "supreamest"</note> faculties and carries with it its own reward: namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, a second youth that is more pleasing and more delightful than the first. 

For while on the one hand, I cannot allow such a person to satisfy, or even feel, his passions <note>strong physical or emotional desires</note>, so on the other hand, I would not want him to be immersed in deep and confusing <note>original: "perplexing"</note> studies. Instead, he should be entertained and distracted by pursuits of a different nature. At certain times, indeed, he might discuss grave and serious topics with his friends; however, I would not want such conversations to occur too frequently, for fear that they might gradually lead him into melancholy. In this context, melancholy is nothing more than focusing the thoughts too intensely on a single object. 

In order to achieve <note>original: "require"</note> and maintain a "green" old age <note>a phrase meaning a healthy, vigorous, and active elderly life</note>, nothing is as necessary as cheerfulness of mind. This state can never be secured if we meditate too much on complex or obscure <note>original: "abstruse"</note> subjects. I do not say that these subjects should always be neglected, but what I mean is that this is not the stage of life in which they ought to be pursued. They require such vigor, such attention—

...meditation, and such a degree of penetration as would lead to such a great waste of spirits <note>original: "Spirits"; in historical medicine, these were vital fluids or energies believed to be essential for life and health</note> that it would defeat the purpose of the remedy I recommend. Therefore, I establish it as a supplemental rule that these activities are to be avoided.

There is, as far as my foresight will carry me, only one more objection that can be raised against my system. This must come from the direction of the politicians, who believe nothing is right or worthy of encouragement unless it aligns with their own notions—notions which are, generally speaking, not very agreeable to those of other men. It would not surprise me, therefore, if these gentlemen should take it into their heads to treat this as a whimsical and trifling performance. I say this because I know that, according to their system, old men are seen as so many burdens <note>original: "Incumbrances"</note> of which the state ought to be relieved. It is upon this principle, I assume, that some Indigenous <note>original: "Indian"</note> nations hold great feasts for their families when their chief becomes decrepit; and when these rejoicings are over, they simply put him out of the way <note>marker: °</note>. <meta>This page continues the author's rebuttal against political thinkers who believe extending the lives of the elderly is a burden to the state. It specifically addresses the argument that "unproductive" members of society do not deserve the resources required to prolong their lives.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>

-fore, must necessarily have a bad opinion (at least while they are young) of such a proposition as this, which intends nothing more than prolonging the lives of those who, in the view of our Machiavellians <note>original: "Machiavelists"; followers of the political philosophy of Niccolò Machiavelli, often characterized here as prioritizing state utility over individual morality</note>, ought to be in their graves already. What benefit, they ask, can result to society from maintaining people past their prime working years <note>original: "past their Labours"</note>, and who, according to this very scheme, are declared unfit for reproduction <note>original: "Propagation"</note>? The public is only benefited by active and industrious persons; why then should so much care be taken to preserve people fit only for an inactive <note>original: "sedentary"</note> life? If the ordinary laws of nature can be set aside, let it be in favor of the public welfare <note>original: "Public Weal"</note>; why should the extravagant desire for life be satisfied, rather than any other extravagant desire? It is evident that reflections like these must induce these "over-wise" persons to treat my book with contempt, whatever opinion they may have of the arguments contained in it. Therefore, it is necessary for me to show that these maxims, however plausible or refined they may appear, are very far from

---

...I find it no difficult matter to distinguish the cause of such an inhuman (I would be wrong to call it merely "brutal") custom. I believe the cause is this: they find it very difficult in that part of the world to acquire the means of survival <note>original: "Subsistance"</note>, and therefore think themselves under a kind of necessity to maintain only those who are useful. This, I am persuaded, was the origin of such a horrid practice. However, it cannot be founded on real necessity, because these people who murder their parents still preserve their children, though they are equally helpless.

<summary>The author challenges the "Machiavellian" view that the elderly are a burden to the state, arguing against the utilitarian logic that would see the aged abandoned or killed. He notes that even in cultures that practice such "horrid" customs due to scarcity, the logic of "utility" is inconsistent since they still care for helpless children.</summary>

...we could increase the number of those who are free from such irregular and depraved appetites <note>original: "Appetites"; here meaning uncontrolled desires or impulses</note>, and who are consequently most capable of conducting both public and private affairs with judgment and integrity. 

It is remarkable that in all well-governed states, a certain maturity of age is required before men are entrusted with the management of not only public concerns, but even their own. Upon what reason, then, is this founded? Is it not because they are thought to lack <note>original: "want"</note>, in the first place, the light of experience, and secondly, to be too much driven by their passions? 

If this is right—and if it is not, then the wisest men in all ages have been wrong—why should we imagine that men have finished their useful work <note>original: "pass'd their Labours"</note> just as they come into possession of those qualities which are certainly the primary ingredients of true wisdom? There will always be enough people in the world full of the heat, vigor, and strength required for carrying on the active and laborious parts of business. However, we see plainly that private families are brought to ruin, and great states gradually decay, for want of that moderation, experience, and foresight usually possessed by those of advanced years whose spirits are not yet oppressed by the infirmities of age.

[The] very nature of it provides against any such inconvenience by requiring such moderation and self-denial as will not frequently be found. It is, therefore, an unjust and unfair accusation <note>original: "imputation"</note> against my principles to say that they tend to burden the world with useless people, since the most that can be claimed is that I am in favor of preserving, for as long as possible, the lives of those who may be useful. 

As to the subsistence of such people, it is easy to see that this would be a very trifling expense when compared even with what they are able to contribute toward the increase of the common wealth <note>original: "common Stock"; refers to the collective resources or productivity of society</note>. So, even when considered in this—which is the very worst light in which the matter can be put—a true statesman <note>original: "politician"</note>, and one who has a proper regard for the interest and a just respect for the rights of mankind, will be far from thinking that I have offered anything which can be harmful to a well-ordered society. Rather, I have provided a very good method for securing a constant supply of such counselors who may preserve society in the best order possible.

<meta>This page continues the author’s citation of Cicero’s "On Old Age" (De Senectute), where Cato the Elder defends the dignity and mental capacity of the elderly against common prejudices of the time.</meta>

->[ 157 ]<-

...beauty and accuracy of style <note>original: "Stile"</note> in which it is delivered. Cato is there answering the very objection which I have been treating. "If," says he, "petulance or lust are vices more frequent among young men than old, yet all young men are not infected with them, but only those who lack proper natural abilities <note>original: "want proper Talents"</note>. So it is with that sort of disorder <note>original: "Distemper"</note> which you call dotage <note>original: "Dotage"; a state of mental decline or senility</note>; it is indeed a disease of old men; but, however, all old men are not infected with it. 

Appius <note>Appius Claudius Caecus, a Roman statesman famous for his speech against peace with Pyrrhus</note> was for some years quite blind <note>original: "dark"</note>, and yet he managed a family of four grown sons and five daughters, along with a vast number of relations and clients who depended upon him. He kept his mind always in order, and though his physical strength <note>original: "Vigour"</note> decayed, his senses never failed him. He preserved his character and his authority to the last moment. Everyone looked up toward him as was appropriate for their station; his slaves feared him, his children revered him, and all who were about him loved him. 

In a word, he maintained the old discipline and did honor to the Roman name by preserving the morals <note>original: "Manners"</note> of his family untainted. Thus, it is plain that old age may maintain a graceful superiority if it is protective <note>original: "jealous"</note> of its prerogatives; if on all occasions it maintains its rights; and if it never sneaks away and gives up, but keeps up a manly spirit to the last. <meta>This page continues the author's argument for the necessity of a long life to achieve intellectual and political greatness, moving from classical examples like Cicero to early modern philosophers and monarchs.</meta>

->[ 160 ]<-

The books of *Aristotle* are considered the most perfect, as are those of *Seneca* which he wrote in his later years; and we may say the same thing regarding modern philosophers, such as *Gassendi*, *Bacon*, and *Newton*. A peaceful old age is therefore of the highest importance in this regard. It contributes to providing the world with new and useful insights <note>original: "Lights"</note> and prevents the public’s expectations—based on the early works of great men—from being disappointed by their premature deaths, as they so often are.

I must also observe that mechanical inventions require much time to perfect. Very profitable discoveries and useful manufacturing processes have been lost because of the deaths of those who first developed <note>original: "Cultivated"</note> them; many examples of this could be given. We may likewise remark that establishing any new colony <note>original: "Plantation"</note>, reforming a society, or, in short, organizing any people who have long lived under a bad form of government (or largely without any government at all) requires a long life.

*Louis* XIV entirely changed the face of affairs in *France* during his reign. This was principally due to the length of his rule, as it gave him the opportunity to see most of his schemes succeed. In contrast, the unexpected death of the Great *Czar Peter* caused many of his projects to be forgotten <note>original: "buried in Oblivion"</note> and must have overturned the whole of his glorious design for re-

We can therefore easily see <note>original: "discern"</note> that such an extension of life as this treatise proposes would be far from burdening the world with a useless race of old men. Instead, it would actually provide the world with the very people who are most needed for the advancement of knowledge, the perfection of mechanical discoveries, and contributing in other ways to the welfare of mankind.

Overall, therefore, I think I can rightly conclude that by examining this ancient inscription, I have not only exercised my thoughts in a way that might entertain <note>original: "Amusement"</note> the learned, but I have also suggested many things that may be of public benefit <note>original: "Utility"</note>. <page-num>160</page-num>

The works of *Aristotle* are considered his most perfect, as are those of *Seneca*, which he wrote in his old age; and we may say the same thing regarding modern philosophers, such as *Gassendi*, *Bacon*, and *Newton*. A peaceful <note>original: "serene"</note> old age is therefore of the highest importance in this regard, since it would help provide the world with new and useful insights <note>original: "Lights"</note>. It would also prevent the public’s high expectations—born from the early works of great men—from being disappointed, as they frequently are, by their premature deaths. I must also observe that mechanical inventions require <note>original: "acquire"</note> much time to perfect, and that very profitable discoveries and very useful manufacturing processes <note>original: "Manufactures"</note> have been lost because of the deaths of those who first developed <note>original: "Cultivated"</note> them, of which many examples could be given. We may also remark that settling any new colony <note>original: "Plantation"</note>, reforming a society, or, in short, bringing order to <note>original: "reducing"</note> any people who have been long under a poor form of government (or largely without any) requires many years <note>original: "length of Days"</note>. *Louis XIV* <note>original: "Lewis XIV."</note> entirely changed the state of affairs in *France* during his reign. This was principally due to the length of it, as it gave him an opportunity to see most of his plans realized <note>original: "brought to bear"</note>. In contrast, the unexpected death of *Peter the Great* <note>original: "the Great Czar Peter"</note> caused many of his projects to be forgotten <note>original: "buried in Oblivion"</note>, and would have overturned his entire glorious design for re-

Overall, therefore, I think I may very rightly conclude that by examining this ancient <note>original: "antient"</note> inscription, I have not only exercised my thoughts in a way that may contribute to the entertainment <note>original: "Amusement"</note> of scholars <note>original: "the Learned"</note>, but that I have also suggested <note>original: "hinted"</note> many things that may be of public benefit <note>original: "Utility"</note>. In this respect, indeed, I think I have made the proper use of the inscription; for it is certainly much better to inform ourselves about practical things rather than just words. My readers would owe me their thanks if I had attempted to discuss whether he lived to be one hundred and fifty-five, or only a hundred and fifteen years old. Have I not chosen the better path by examining what may still be of some use—namely <note>original: "viz."</note>—the method by which he reached such a great age, and how practical it is for us to follow his method? I think I may go even further and affirm that I have made it at least extremely probable that the *breath of young girls* <note>original: "Anhelitus Puellarum"</note> is, in some measure, the "breath of life." To achieve this, I hope I have effectively dismantled some of the prejudices that affected the minds of even sensible people regarding the fixed limit of human life and the supposed impossibility of prolonging it through science <note>original: "Art"</note>.

I have likewise, in the course of this dissertation, collected the views of several great men who think—or at least seem to me to think—in the same way that I do. By doing so, I have given the curious and inquisitive reader an opportunity to search more closely into their opinions to ensure they are treated fairly, in case, after careful consideration, the reader thinks that I have failed to do so.

If this contributes only a little to clarifying the present point, it might still serve as a means of bringing many significant matters to light. We have shown, through our citations from old books, that many of the discoveries for which modern thinkers <note>original: "the Moderns"</note> are famous were actually...

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that ancient civilizations possessed significant knowledge and skills that have since been lost or forgotten, challenging the modern assumption of intellectual superiority.</meta>

known to the learned in former times. From this, we may probably conjecture that some other things were known to them with which we are not acquainted. This is especially likely because there is hardly any country where ancient monuments are found where we cannot discern in them marks of skill and capacity that transcend the power of modern workmen. And though these works may not suit our current taste—which seems to be a polite expression for the prevailing whims of mankind, which change too often to deserve the name of a rule—they still manifest great quickness of invention and the possession of many arts that are now lost. It reflects well on antiquity that the oldest building in the world, which is the Temple of *Theseus* at *Athens* <note>Now more commonly known as the Temple of Hephaestus; it is one of the best-preserved ancient Greek temples.</note>, is by far the finest. In literature as well, *Homer* and *Hippocrates* plainly prove that, in terms of genius or of industry, the early ages remain unrivaled. 

There are several roads which lead to true wisdom; we may sometimes reach it by looking backward as well as forward, and profit as much by detecting the prejudices of modern thinkers <note>original: "the Moderns"</note> as by refuting the errors of antiquity. It is a just observation by the great *Lord Verulam* <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626), 1st Viscount St Alban and Lord Verulam, an influential philosopher often credited with developing the scientific method.</note> that having a lot of learning frees us from those mistakes into which we are plunged by having only a little. <meta>This page continues the author's discussion from page 163 regarding the shift in how modern scholars view ancient writings, moving from skepticism to a newfound respect for their insights.</meta>

nothing but absurdities and improbabilities. But now that we are better acquainted with experimental philosophy <note>original: "Experimental Philosophy"; this refers to the early scientific method based on observation and experimentation rather than just theory.</note>, we begin to have a better opinion of these authors and are obliged to confess that, in some cases, keen insight <note>original: "sagacity"</note> may make up for a lack of direct experience. I am, however, ready to admit that in some branches of science, we have significantly surpassed the ancients; but what of that? We are still far short of perfection. 

Our very discoveries prove it. For example, we discern the effects of gravity and see the whole mechanism of the universe depending upon it, but the cause has been, until now, inexplicable. We are well acquainted with the many properties of the compass needle <note>original: "the Needle"</note>, which were secrets to former times, but the cause of its variation <note>Magnetic declination, or the way a compass needle deviates from true north depending on its location.</note> remains a secret to us. We have, indeed, made some bold guesses at both, and it is not impossible that future generations <note>original: "Posterity"</note> may be able to demonstrate our conjectures; just as it is, on the other hand, also possible that they may prove them to be only idle conjectures. 

If we would avoid being deceived, we must reject bigotry <note>Here used in the sense of narrow-mindedness or intellectual dogmatism.</note> of all sorts. We must not carry our veneration for the ancients too far, and at the same time, we ought not to be afraid of using a just freedom with the moderns <note>The scientists and thinkers of the author's own time.</note>; since it is not our business to admire others, but to instruct ourselves.

With the same view of reaching the truth, by setting the subject of which I treat in different lights <note>The original text ends here with the catchword "Lights," signaling the start of the next page.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the author's concluding remarks on his investigation into life extension, shifting from a critique of modern and ancient thinkers to a summary of his own methods and the evidence he has provided.</meta>

Thus, <note>OCR reads "Lights," but context suggests a transition such as "Thus" or "In this light."</note> I have stated the opinions of others fairly and have reasoned upon them as freely as I desire to see my own system treated. I have given the reader the views of astrologers and of Hermetic philosophers <note>Followers of a mystical and philosophical tradition centered on alchemy and secret knowledge, attributed to the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus.</note> regarding this inscription. I have left it to the reader to decide whether they are more correct than I, or whether we may all be mistaken and this inscription possesses, after all, some other secret and even more probable meaning than any of us have reached.

To encourage a thorough discussion of this topic, I have often emphasized <note>original: "inculcated"</note> the usefulness of such an inquiry. I believe I have fully proved, beyond the possibility of a reply, that it is very practical to extend the thread of life <note>A metaphor for the span of a human life, referring to the "thread" spun by the Fates in classical mythology.</note> beyond the length it usually reaches. If this is true, it will be a real benefit and not an idle or trifling discovery.

These are the principal points I have worked on. To avoid that heaviness and boredom <note>original: "dryness"</note> commonly complained about in academic works <note>original: "Discourses"</note> of this kind, I have frequently taken "excursions" for the entertainment of myself and my readers. I have inserted an abundance of curious passages from rare and valuable books, which I hope will make full amends for the trouble of reading this treatise <note>A formal and systematic written work on a subject.</note>—even for those who may remain unconvinced of the truth or practicality of my system. At least I can say this for my book and for myself: nothing has been omitted within the reach of my power which

Such people value themselves much more for exposing the faults of other men than for showing any valuable qualities in themselves; they are better pleased to destroy a building <note>original: "Edifice"</note> erected by another than to earn a just reputation by raising a better structure of their own.

There is one more thing I must remark, and it is this: my system is entirely on the right side. I have not undertaken to demonstrate a paradox that goes against <note>original: "in the Teeth of"</note> common sense and the common interest of the human species, as many others have done. The famous *Praise of Folly* <note>original: "Encomium on Folly," a satirical essay written by Desiderius Erasmus in 1509.</note> and the *Panegyric on Drunkenness* <note>original: "Panegyric on Drunkenness," likely referring to the satirical work *Ebrietatis Encomium* by Albert-Henri de Sallengre (1714).</note> are glaring instances of such paradoxes. Instead, what I have laid down in this discourse is intended for public utility and from a desire of doing good. If, therefore, upon thoroughly sifting this dispute, the matter should come at last to be in balance <note>original: *Equilibrio*</note>, I hope the turn of the scales will be allowed to me. For surely, if wisdom <note>original: "Sense"</note> is preferable to senility <note>original: "Dotage"</note>, comfort <note>original: "Ease"</note> to pain, or life to death, I have a fair claim to this favor. <meta>This page continues the author's defense of his work. He argues that new ideas are often met with unfair prejudice and appeals to the reader's goodwill by citing the examples of William Harvey and Isaac Newton.</meta>

...this way <note>The text continues from the previous page's thought: "...surely, if wisdom [either prompts or allows me to seek] this way..."</note>, the inclinations or prejudices of my readers in my favor, for that might prove detrimental to truth; but I introduce this remark to protect myself against the effects of an irritable <note>original: "Petulant"</note> temperament <note>original: "Humour"</note> which is all too common in the world: the habit of disliking whatever has the appearance of novelty and assuming that there is something equally just and wise in defending old opinions against new ones and in attacking <note>original: "running down"</note> any system as soon as it appears. 

Against this unfair <note>original: "unequitable"</note> bias <note>original: "Prepossession"</note> in favor of ancient ideas, I present this claim for the goodwill of men, based on the benefits resulting from my system to the human race, whether considered as individuals or as a society. It was upon the same principle that the late *Dr. Harvey* <note>William Harvey (1578–1657), the English physician who first described the systemic circulation of blood.</note> established his doctrine of the circulation of the blood, which, as he rightly observed, would free the art of medicine <note>original: "Physic"</note> from many criticisms and enable us to give a clearer and more rational account of the animal system <note>original: "Animal Oeconomy," a traditional term referring to the organization and functioning of a living body.</note>. 

On this basis, too, stands the philosophy of *Sir Isaac Newton*, which is perhaps the noblest achievement of human understanding. I do not mean by this to compare my invention with theirs, or myself to either of those great men. All I aim to do is show that even the greatest men have needed patience <note>original: "Indulgence"</note> when they proposed new systems; from which I conclude <note>original: "infer"</note> that such a request cannot be thought [either]...
